SPT indices emerging from translation invariance in two-dimensional quantum spin systems
Tijl Jappens

Abstract
We consider SPT-phases with on-site G G (where G G is any finite group) symmetry for two-dimensional quantum spin systems. We then impose translation invariance in one direction and observe that on top of the H 3 ​ ( G , 𝕋 ) H^{3}(G,\mathbb{T}) -valued index constructed in [ 10 ] , an additional H 2 ​ ( G , 𝕋 ) H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T}) -valued index emerges. We also show that if we impose translation invariance in two directions, on top of the expected H 3 ​ ( G , 𝕋 ) ⊕ H 2 ​ ( G , 𝕋 ) ⊕ H 2 ​ ( G , 𝕋 ) H^{3}(G,\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T}) valued index, an additional H 1 ​ ( G , 𝕋 ) H^{1}(G,\mathbb{T}) -valued index emerges.



1
Introduction

In 2010, Chen, Gu and Wen
[
4
]
introduced the notion of symmetry-protected topological phases of matter (SPT). They first considered one-dimensional matrix product states and later on, Chen, Liu and Wen
[
5
]
, extended this to two-dimensional projective entangled pair models. Afterwards, in
[
3
]
this concept was extended to other dimensions. This last reference used a setup involving so-called lattice non-linear sigma models and provided an
H^{d+1}(G,U(1))
valued index for any
d
-dimensional lattice non-linear sigma model. The standard setup for defining SPT phases is in quantum spin systems (defined in section
2.1
). To define SPT phases in this setting, one needs to fix a finite group
G
with an on-site group action
g\mapsto U_{g}
(with
g\in G
), defining a global group action
\beta_{g}
(also defined in section
2.1
). A state
\omega
, is then called
G
-invariant, if
\omega\circ\beta_{g}=\omega
. Afterwards, one needs to restrict the space of states. We
1
1

1

There are other definitions possible like for instance the concept of invertible G-invariant states used in
[
6
]
or the unique gapped ground state of a G invariant interaction presented in the introduction of
[
10
]
.
ask that our states are short-range entangled (SRE), see definition
2.2
. A state
\omega
is short-range entangled if there is a disentangler
\gamma
. This is a locally generated automorphism, produced by a one-parameter family of interactions
\Phi\in\mathcal{B}_{F_{\phi}}([0,1])
(
\gamma=\gamma_{0;1}^{\Phi}
as defined in section
2.3
) satisfying that
\omega\circ\gamma
is a product state. Combining these two definitions, we say that
\omega
is an SPT state if it is short-range entangled and
G
-invariant.

One then defines an equivalence relation on these SPT states by saying that
\omega_{1}
is equivalent to
\omega_{2}
with respect to the on-site group action
U
if there exists a
G
-invariant one-parameter family of interactions
\Phi\in\mathcal{B}_{F_{\phi}}([0,1])
such that
\omega_{1}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}=\omega_{2}
(again, see section
2.3
for the definitions). One can then extend this equivalence to the stronger notion of being stably equivalent. This goes as follows: one defines an operation called stacking that takes two SPT states
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
and outputs a third one
\omega_{1}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\omega_{2}
(and similarly for the group action). This
\otimes_{\text{stack}}
is defined in section
2.1
. In short, it is the tensor product at the level of the on-site Hilbert space. One then defines another equivalence relation and says that two SPT states with on-site group actions
(\omega_{1},U_{1})
and
(\omega_{2},U_{2})
are stably equivalent if and only if there exist trivial
2
2

2

A trivial state is a
G
-invariant product state that transforms trivially under the on-site group action (see remark
7.1
).
SPTs with their group actions,
(\phi_{1},\tilde{U}_{1})
and
(\phi_{2},\tilde{U}_{2})
and a (
G
independent) unitary
V
mapping between the respective on-site Hilbert spaces
3
3

3

The reader can think of this
V
as being a unitary transformation of the on-site group action.
, such that

1.

V^{\dagger}U_{1,g}\otimes\tilde{U}_{1,g}V=U_{2,g}\otimes\tilde{U}_{2,g}
for all
g\in G
.

2.

\omega_{1}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\phi_{1}\circ i_{V}
(where
i_{V}
is defined in section
2.1
) is equivalent to
\omega_{2}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\phi_{2}
with respect to the above on-site group action.

In one spatial dimension so-called matrix product states (
[
5
]
,
[
11
]
,
[
12
]
) can be used to construct non-trivial SPT states. Later on and more generally it was shown in
[
9
]
that any
G
-invariant state satisfying the split property (this includes any SRE state) carries an
H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})
-valued
4
4

4

By
H^{n}(G,\mathbb{T})
we mean the n-th (Borel) group cohomology of
G
with coefficients in the torus
\mathbb{T}
(seen as a (group) module under addition with trivial group action).
index and that this index is constant on the (stable) equivalence classes. Later on in
[
6
]
it was then shown that this classification problem is complete (the index, seen as a map from the set of stable equivalence classes to
H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})
is injective.). In two spatial dimensions, more recently in
[
10
]
, it was proven there is an
H^{3}(G,\mathbb{T})
-valued index that is constant on the (stable) equivalence classes
5
5

5

In
[
10
]
, there is no mention of the stacking operation but using remark
11.3
and remark
12.3
, one can extend it to the stable equivalence class.
. In this paper, we will denote this
H^{3}(G,\mathbb{T})
-valued index of a state
\omega
by
\textrm{Index}_{\text{2D}}(\omega)
.

We’ve used stacking to define the stable equivalence relation. Stacking however plays a more general role. It is widely believed that it allows us to define an (abelian) group structure on the set of stable equivalence classes
6
6

6

The stacking with a trivial state will then be related to the identity of this group
. Although it is nowhere stated explicitly, the author thinks that the literature (like, for example,
[
6
]
) suggests the following conjecture:

Conjecture 1.1
.

Take some fixed group and a fixed lattice. Let
S
be the set of stable SPT classes defined through the above equivalence class. Let
*
be the map

*:S^{2}\rightarrow S:(\expectationvalue{\omega_1,U_1}_{\sim},\expectationvalue{\omega_2,U_2}_{\sim})\mapsto\expectationvalue{\omega_1\otimes_\text{stack}\omega_2,U_1\otimes U_2}_{\sim}.

(1.1)

Then
*
is well-defined (independent of the choice of representative) and
S
equipped with the
*
operation forms an abelian group.

Before proceeding, we make three remarks on this conjecture:

1.

The claim that
*
is well-defined and abelian is trivial. Namely, by letting
i_{V}
be the automorphism that exchanges the two algebras that are being stacked, one can exchange the order of the stacking freely. By using

\omega_{1}\circ\gamma^{\Phi_{1}}_{0;1}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\omega_{2}\circ\gamma^{\Phi_{2}}_{0;1}=(\omega_{1}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\omega_{2})\circ\gamma^{\Phi_{1}\otimes\mathbbm{1}+\mathbbm{1}\otimes\Phi_{2}}_{0;1}\sim\omega_{1}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\omega_{2},

(1.2)

one can show that this is a well-defined map.

2.

A representative of the inverse is sometimes called a
G
-inverse.

3.

From a category point of view, if one assumes that this conjecture is true (for some subclass of groups that is a category), it is not hard to see that it gives rise to a contravariant functor from this category of groups to the category of abelian groups (say
S[G]
). More specifically, to each group morphism
f:G_{1}\rightarrow G_{2}
, one can find a group morphism
\tilde{f}:S[G_{2}]\rightarrow S[G_{1}]:\expectationvalue{\omega,U}_{\sim}\mapsto\expectationvalue{\omega,U\circ f}_{\sim}
and this
\tilde{f}
is indeed invariant on the choice of representative (if two states can be connected while preserving the symmetry action
\beta
, they can certainly be connected while preserving the symmetry
\beta\circ f
).

In what follows, we will call
S
with the above group structure the stable SPT classification. In this paper, we consider the 2D case with the (stable) equivalence relation as presented here but with one notable difference. We will only consider states, interactions, and on-site symmetries that have a translation symmetry in one direction. There is a conjecture about the SPT classification of such states, see section 3.1.5.1 of
[
13
]
for a more general and detailed exposition (which was partially based on
[
3
]
).

Conjecture 1.2
.

The set of translation invariant SPTs under stable equivalence
7
7

7

In the stable equivalence relation for translation invariant states we ask that the on-site symmetry is translation invariant and that any path connecting two states and each trivial state is not only G-invariant but also translation invariant.
also satisfies conjecture
1.1
. Let
g
be the inclusion map from the space of 2d translation invariant SPT states to the more general set of 2d SPT states. Let
f
be the map that takes a 1d SPT state and outputs a translation invariant 2d SPT state by taking the tensor product in the direction of the translation symmetry. The sequence

0\rightarrow\{\text{1d SPT states}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}\{\text{2d translation invariant SPT states}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\rightarrow}}\{\text{2d SPT states}\}\rightarrow 0

(1.3)

induces a sequence (of group morphisms) on the stable equivalence classes. By this, we mean that the class of
f(\phi)
only depends on the class of
\phi
and similarly for
g
. Moreover, this induced sequence is exact and split.

This implies that

\{\text{2d translation invariant SPT classification}\}\simeq\{\text{1d SPT classification}\}\oplus\{\text{2d SPT classification}\}.

(1.4)

Since a 1d SPT state carries an
H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})
valued index and a 2d SPT state carries an
H^{3}(G,\mathbb{T})
valued index, this means that 2d SPT states with a translation symmetry should carry an
H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{3}(G,\mathbb{T})
valued index. This is consistent with the result of section XII of
[
3
]
.

The first result of this paper is the construction of an
H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})
valued index (which we will denote by
Index
) on the space of translation invariant SPT states that is consistent with conjectures
1.1
and
1.2
. By being consistent with the conjecture it is meant (among other things) that, if
\textrm{Index}_{1}
is the one-dimensional SPT index from
[
9
]
, then
\textrm{Index}(f(\omega))=\textrm{Index}_{1}(\omega)
for any 1d SPT
\omega
. This construction highly relies on the objects that are present in the construction of the 2d SPT index in
[
10
]
.

We also look at the case where there is a second translation symmetry (so the system is translation invariant in both
x
and
y
directions). For such systems, there is the following conjecture (see again
[
13
]
and
[
3
]
):

Conjecture 1.3
.

The space of SPTs, translation invariant in two directions under stable equivalence also satisfies conjecture
1.1
. Let
g
be the inclusion map from the space of 2d SPT states that are translation invariant in both
x
and
y
directions to the more general set of 2d SPT states with translation invariance in the
x
direction only. Let
f
be the map that takes a 1d translation invariant SPT state and outputs a 2d SPT state that is translation invariant in both
x
and
y
directions by taking the tensor product in the
y
direction. The sequence

0\rightarrow\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{matrix}\text{1d translation invariant}\\
\text{SPT states}\end{matrix}}}\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{matrix}\text{2d SPT states with two}\\
\text{translation symmetries}\end{matrix}}}\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{matrix}\text{2d SPT states translation}\\
\text{invariant in $x$ direction}\end{matrix}}}\right\}\rightarrow 0

(1.5)

induces a sequence (of group morphisms) on the stable equivalence classes. By this, we mean that the class of
f(\phi)
only depends on the class of
\phi
and similarly for
g
. Moreover, this induced sequence is exact and split.

Translation invariant SPT states in one spatial dimension are known to carry an
H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{1}(G,\mathbb{T})
valued index (see section
4
for a construction of these indices consistent with the rest of the setup). From this and the last two conjectures, we expect there to be an

H^{3}(G,\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{1}(G,\mathbb{T})

(1.6)

valued index. The first part of the index is just the above mentioned
\textrm{Index}_{2d}
. The following two parts can be related to the case of a single translation symmetry as follows. Let
\mu
be the automorphism that rotates the lattice by 90° and let
\textrm{Index}(\omega)
be the index as constructed before (this is the second part of
1.6
). The third part of the index is now given by
\textrm{Index}(\omega\circ\mu)
(the state rotated by 90°). The last part of the index requires a different construction altogether. It can be thought of as being the charge in the Brillouin zone.

A final remark:

Remark 1.4
.

As noticed in
[
3
]
. The groups in which these indices take values can be concisely written out. Using that

\displaystyle H^{0}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{T})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle H^{1}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{T})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle H^{2}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{T})

\displaystyle\cong 0

\displaystyle H^{3}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{T})

\displaystyle\cong 0

(1.7)

and inserting this into the Künneth formula for
\mathbb{Z}\times G
gives

\displaystyle H^{2}(\mathbb{Z}\times G,\mathbb{T})

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{i=0}^{2}H^{i}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{T})\otimes H^{2-i}(G,\mathbb{T})\cong H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{1}(G,\mathbb{T})

(1.8)

\displaystyle H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}\times G,\mathbb{T})

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{i=0}^{3}H^{i}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{T})\otimes H^{3-i}(G,\mathbb{T})\cong H^{3}(G,\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T}).

(1.9)

This result can in turn be used to work out the Künneth formula for
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\times G

H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\times G,\mathbb{T})\cong\bigoplus_{i=0}^{3}H^{i}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{T})\otimes H^{3-i}(\mathbb{Z}\times G,\mathbb{T})\cong H^{3}(G,\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{1}(G,\mathbb{T}).

(1.10)

The layout of this paper is as follows: First in section
2
we explain the setup, define the concept of locally generated automorphisms, and give the algebraic definition of group cohomology. In section
3.2
we state the two results for SRE states. In section
3.1
, we state the first result using the weaker assumption
3.2
and the second result using the weaker assumption
3.5
. It is using these weaker assumptions that we then prove the statements. The rest of the paper provides a proof of these statements. The results in this paper rely heavily on the methods that were developed in
[
10
]
.

Setup and definitions

In this paper, we work in the two-dimensional lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. We will first need some specific subsets of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, so let

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|x<0}}\right.}}\right\},

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|x\geq 0}}\right.}}\right\}

(2.1)

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|y\geq 0}}\right.}}\right\},

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|y<0}}\right.}}\right\}

(2.2)

be the left, right, upper, and lower half-planes respectively, and let

C_{\theta}\vcentcolon=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\tan(\theta)\leq\absolutevalue{\frac{y}{x}}}}\right.}}\right\}

(2.3)

be the horizontal cone (the green area in figure
1
). We will use
\tau
to denote the bijection that moves every element of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
one site upward. Similarly, we let
\nu
denote the bijection that translates every element of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
by one site to the right. In what follows we will sometimes need to widen our cone or other subsets of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
vertically by one site. For this purpose we define
W
such that for any
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,

W(\Gamma)\vcentcolon=\Gamma\cup\tau(\Gamma)\cup\tau^{-1}(\Gamma).

(2.4)

For example, the red area in figure
1
is
W(C_{\theta})^{c}
. In the later part, we will also need to rotate our lattice by
90^{\circ}
(clockwise) so call
\mu
the bijection on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
that does precisely this.
8
8

8

We will not discuss rotation invariant states in this paper. The
\mu
is only defined to make certain constructions work.
Finally, we will need to define the horizontal lines and the finite horizontal line

\displaystyle L_{j}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{(i,j)|i\in\mathbb{Z}\}

\displaystyle L_{j}^{n}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{(i,j)|i\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[-n,n]\}.

(2.5)

2.1
Quasi local
C^{*}
-algebra

The setup in this paper will be very similar to the setup in
[
10
]
and is just the standard setup for defining quantum spin systems. For the rest of this paper, take
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
arbitrary. This number will be called the on-site dimension
9
9

9

In principle in the case where there is only one translation we can let
d
be
x
-dependent but for simplicity let us take
d
constant over the lattice.
. For each
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, let
\mathcal{A}_{\{z\}}
be an isomorphic copy of
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
(the bounded operators on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
). In what follows, let
\mathfrak{G}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
be the set of finite subsets of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. For any
\Lambda\in\mathfrak{G}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
, we set
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}=\bigotimes_{z\in\Lambda}\mathcal{A}_{\{z\}}
. We define the local algebra as
\mathcal{A}_{\text{loc}}=\bigcup_{\Lambda\in\mathfrak{G}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}}\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}
and define the quasi local
C^{*}
algebra as the norm closure of the local algebra (
\mathcal{A}=\overline{\mathcal{A}_{\text{loc}}}
). Similarly, for any (possibly infinite) subset
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
we set
\mathcal{A}_{\text{loc},\Gamma}=\bigcup_{\Lambda\in\mathfrak{G}_{\Gamma}}\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}=\overline{\mathcal{A}_{\text{loc},\Gamma}}
.

Throughout this paper, we will sometimes require a stronger form of locality than merely being in the norm completion of the local algebra. Specifically when
\Gamma=L_{j}
is a horizontal line. We will say that an operator
A\in\mathcal{A}_{L_{j}}
is summable if and only if there exists a sequence
A_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{L_{j}^{n}}
on the finite horizontal lines such that

\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\norm{A-A_n}<\infty.

(2.6)

We will need to define some automorphisms on this algebra using the bijections defined previously. To this end, define the isomorphisms

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle:\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\tau(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle:\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\nu(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle:\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mu(\Gamma)}

(2.7)

as the translation upwards, the translation to the right, and the right-handed rotation respectively. By construction, these isomorphisms are automorphisms if
\Gamma
is taken to be
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
(because
d
is a constant throughout the lattice). Clearly if
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}
(by which we mean again the
x
-axis
(\mathbb{Z},0)
) only one of these translation automorphisms descends to an automorphism on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. This automorphism is then also simply denoted as
\nu
.

We will need to define one additional class of automorphisms. For any set of unitaries
V_{i}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
(labelled by
i\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
), we define the (unique) automorphism
i_{V}^{\Gamma}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}}}\right)
(for all
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
) such that
i_{V}^{\Gamma}(A)=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\otimes_{i\in I}V_{i}}}\right)(A)
for all
I\subset\Gamma
finite and
A\in\mathcal{A}_{I}
. This class of automorphisms will be used to define a group action on the algebra. More specifically, let
G
be a finite group and let
U_{i}\in\hom(G,\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{i}))
(for
i\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
) be the on-site group action. Let
\beta^{\Gamma}\in\hom(G,\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}}}\right))
(for any
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
) be such that

\beta^{\Gamma}_{g}(A)=i_{U(g)}^{\Gamma}=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\otimes_{i\in I}U_{i}(g)}}\right)(A)

(2.8)

for any
g\in G
,
I\subset\Gamma
finite and
A\in\mathcal{A}_{I}
. Clearly, any unitary transformation of our representation
U(\cdot)\rightarrow V^{\dagger}U(\cdot)V
induces a transformation of the group action
\beta_{g}\rightarrow i_{V}^{-1}\circ\beta_{g}\circ i_{V}
.

Throughout our paper when we are discussing translation invariance in the vertical direction we will ask that the group action is translation invariant in the vertical direction (
\tau\circ\beta_{g}^{\Gamma}=\beta_{g}^{\tau(\Gamma)}\circ\tau
). If we also have translation invariance in the horizontal direction, we will also ask that the group action be translation invariant in that direction as well (
\nu\circ\beta_{g}^{\Gamma}=\beta_{g}^{\nu(\Gamma)}\circ\nu
). As it turns out, in showing that the
H^{1}
-valued index is consistent with all the conjectures of the introduction, we will even require that the on-site group action
U_{i}(g)
is the same at each site. In what follows we will always assume this. This means that when we denote an on-site symmetry action we merely have to give one representation
U\in\hom(G,\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{C}^{d}))
, and when we denote a unitary transformation of the on-site Hilbert space we only have to give a single unitary
V\in\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
, we will use this notation frequently.

By construction, there is a tensor product operation on this
C^{*}
algebra in the sense that for any
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
satisfying that
\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2}=\emptyset
we can define a bilinear, surjective map

\otimes:\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma_{1}}\times\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma_{2}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}}.

(2.9)

There is however a second tensor product operation on this
C^{*}
algebra that we will use. We will call it the stacking operation. It is such that for any
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
we define the bilinear, surjective map

\otimes_{\text{stack}}:\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}\times\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{2}_{\Gamma}

(2.10)

where
\mathcal{A}^{2}
is the quasi-local
C^{*}
algebra on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
constructed from an on-site algebra
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{C}^{d})\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{C}^{d})\simeq\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{C}^{d\times d})
10
10

10

This can also be used to stack two
C^{*}
-algebras with different on-site Hilbert spaces, this was used in the introduction. In the rest of the paper we will for simplicity of notation (but without loss of generality) only stack two identical
C^{*}
-algebras on top of each other.
. We will also need to define the stacking of the group action by which we mean

(\beta_{g}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\beta_{g})^{\Lambda}=\bigotimes_{z\in\Lambda}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(U(g)\otimes U(g)_{z}}}\right)

(2.11)

for any
\Lambda\in\mathfrak{G}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
(in fact stacking can be defined for any automorphism, not just the group action).

2.2
States on
\mathcal{A}

We will use
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
to denote the set of pure states on
\mathcal{A}
(see
[
2
]
). In this paper, we will often refer to the GNS triple. For its definition, construction, and properties we refer to
[
2
]
. We will sometimes write the tensor product of states
\omega\otimes\phi
to be such that
(\omega\otimes\phi)(a\otimes b)=\omega(a)\phi(b)
and similarly for the stacking tensor product. We will call a pure state
\phi
on
\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}
a product state if for any
\Lambda\subset\Gamma
, the restriction
\phi|_{\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}}
is still pure. We will call a state
\omega
,
G
-invariant if
\omega\circ\beta_{g}=\omega
(where
\beta_{g}=i_{U(g)}
with
U
an on site group action). Moreover, we say that a
G
-invariant product state
\phi
is trivial with respect to
U
(we will sometimes denote this as
(\phi,U)
is trivial) if
\phi(U_{i}(g))=1
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

There is a natural way to construct a pure
G
-invariant state on the quasi local
C^{*}
-algebra over
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
using a pure
G
-invariant state on the quasi local
C^{*}
-algebra over
\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathcal{A}_{L_{0}}
11
11

11

When applied to a trivial state on
\mathcal{A}_{L_{0}}
this will also give a trivial state on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
.
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\phi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A}_{L_{0}})
be a state that is
G
-invariant under a group action
\beta_{g}^{L_{0}}
. Define
\phi_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A}_{L_{i}})
as
\phi_{i}:=\phi_{0}\circ\tau^{-i}
. We define the infinite tensor product state
\omega_{\phi}
as
\omega_{\phi}:=\bigotimes_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\phi_{j}
. This is a
G
-invariant pure state over
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
that is invariant under the automorphism
\tau
.

The proof that this is a well-defined state is done in definition
8.2
.

2.3
Interactions and locally generated automorphisms

An interaction
\Phi
is a map

\Phi:\mathfrak{G}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\text{loc}}:I\mapsto\Phi(I)

(2.12)

where
\Phi(I)\in\mathcal{A}_{I}
is hermitian (
\Phi(I)=\Phi(I)^{*}
). We will sometimes use the restriction of an interaction,
\Phi_{\Gamma}
for
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. This means setting all
\Phi(I)=0
when
I
is not in
\Gamma
. Following the work done in
[
8
]
, will use
\norm{\Phi}_{F}
to indicate the
F
-norm of
\Phi
where
F
is an
F
-function. We will use
\mathcal{B}_{F}([0,1])
to denote the one-parameter families of interactions with norm continuous therms and uniformly bounded
F
-norms. Just as was done in
[
8
]
, for any
\Phi\in\mathcal{B}_{F}([0,1])
, we use
\gamma^{\Phi}_{s;t}
to denote the locally generated automorphism (LGA) generated by
\Phi
from
s
to
t
. Following
[
10
]
, we will fix a specific family of monotonically decreasing positive functions by saying that for any
0<\phi<1
we define

F_{\phi}:\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}:r\mapsto\frac{\exp(-r^\phi)}{(1+r)^{4}}.

(2.13)

This
F
-function is particularly useful to us because, as was shown in
[
10
]
, if
\Phi\in\mathcal{B}_{F_{\phi}}([0,1])
, we can decompose the automorphism into different parts. More precisely, we can show that
\gamma^{\Phi}_{s;t}\in\textrm{QAut}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
where
\textrm{QAut}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
is defined in
3.1
12
12

12

In appendix
A
we show a stronger version of this statement.
.

Sometimes we will say that an interaction is
G
-invariant. By this we simply mean that
\beta_{g}(\Phi(I))=\Phi(I)
(for all
I\in\mathfrak{G}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
). Similarly, if we say an interaction is translation invariant we mean that
\tau(\Phi(I))=\Phi(\tau(I))
(for all
I\in\mathfrak{G}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
). It should be clear that if an interaction is
G
-invariant (translation invariant), then the LGA it generates commutes with the group action (the translation automorphism) as well.

2.4
The stable equivalence class

We will now define a subset of these states that we will call short-range entangled (SRE):

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\omega\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
. We say that
\omega
is an SRE state if and only if there exists a
\phi\in]0,1[
such that there exists an interaction
\Phi\in\mathcal{B}_{F_{\phi}}([0,1])
such that
\omega\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}
is a product state. We then call
\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}
a disentangler for
\omega
.

On the set of
G
-invariant SRE states we will now define three stable equivalence classes depending on the additional imposed symmetry:

Definition 2.3
.

Take
(\omega_{1},U_{1})
and
(\omega_{2},U_{2})
elements of

S_{0}=\{(\omega,U)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})\times\hom(G,U(\mathbb{C}^{d}))|\omega\text{ is SRE},\>\omega\circ i_{U(g)}=\omega\>\forall g\in G\}

(2.14)

for two algebras
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
13
13

13

With two different algebras we simply mean two different on-site dimensions because we already fixed each on-site dimension to be identical.
. We say that
\expectationvalue{\omega_1,U_1}_{\sim}=\expectationvalue{\omega_2,U_2}_{\sim}
if and only if there exists a
\phi\in]0,1[
such that there exists a
G
-invariant interaction
\Phi\in\mathcal{B}_{F_{\phi}}([0,1])
, trivial elements of
S_{0}
(see subsection
2.2
),
(\phi_{1},\tilde{U}_{1})
and
(\phi_{2},\tilde{U}_{2})
for algebras
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
respectively and an on-site unitary such that

\displaystyle\omega_{1}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\phi_{1}\circ i_{V}

\displaystyle=\omega_{2}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\phi_{2}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}

\displaystyle V^{\dagger}U_{1}(g)\otimes\tilde{U}_{1}(g)V

\displaystyle=U_{2}(g)\otimes\tilde{U}_{2}(g).

(2.15)

We also define similar equivalence classes
\expectationvalue{\cdot}_{\sim}^{1},\expectationvalue{\cdot}_{\sim}^{2}
and
\expectationvalue{\cdot}_{\sim}^{3}
on

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle=\{(\omega,U)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})\times\hom(G,U(\mathbb{C}^{d}))|\omega\text{ is SRE},\>\omega\circ i_{U(g)}=\omega\>\forall g\in G,\>\omega\circ\tau=\omega\}

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle=\{(\omega,U)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})\times\hom(G,U(\mathbb{C}^{d}))|\omega\text{ is SRE},\>\omega\circ i_{U(g)}=\omega\>\forall g\in G,\>\omega\circ\nu=\omega\}

(2.16)

\displaystyle S_{3}

\displaystyle=\{(\omega,U)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})\times\hom(G,U(\mathbb{C}^{d}))|\omega\text{ is SRE},\>\omega\circ i_{U(g)}=\omega\>\forall g\in G,\>\omega\circ\tau=\omega,\>\omega\circ\nu=\omega\}

respectively. Here we now also demand that the interactions and the trivial states are invariant under the additional translation symmetries.

3
Results

To formulate the results, we will often use
H^{n}(G,\mathbb{T})
to denote the n-th (Borel) group cohomology of
G
with coefficients in the torus
\mathbb{T}
(seen as a (group) module under addition with trivial group action). For the definition of these group cohomology modules, we refer to
[
1
]
.

This section will contain three different parts. First, we discuss the main result starting from some rather abstract assumptions. In the second part, we present the claim that these assumptions are implied by some more natural assumptions relating to short-range entanglement. In the last part of the section we compare our assumptions to those made in
[
10
]
.

3.1
Statement of the result in terms of Q-automorphisms

\alpha_{L}
\alpha_{R}

\theta
\eta^{L}_{g}
\eta^{R}_{g}
\Theta

Figure 1
:
These figures indicate the support area of the different automorphisms in a particular decomposition of the
QAut
. The angle
\theta
needs to be smaller than or equal to what was indicated here so that the
\Theta
and the
\eta_{g}
(possibly after widening by one site vertically and one site horizontally) commute.

Similarly what was done in

, we will define an index on the space of states that can be disentangled by a Q-automorphism. This class of automorphisms is defined as:

Definition 3.1
.

Take
\alpha\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
. We say that
\alpha\in\textrm{QAut}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
if and only if
\forall\theta\in]0,\pi/2[
there exists an
\alpha_{L}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{L}}}\right),\alpha_{R}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{R}}}\right),V_{1}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
and a
\Theta\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{W(C_{\theta})^{c}}}}\right)
14
14

14

Note that our definition of
\textrm{QAut}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
is slightly different from the definition of
\textrm{QAut}(\mathcal{A})
from
[

]
because of this vertical widening
W
.
such that

\alpha=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{1}}}\right)\circ\alpha_{L}\otimes\alpha_{R}\circ\Theta.

(3.1)

We will often write
\alpha_{0}
to mean
\alpha_{L}\otimes\alpha_{R}
. In this paper, we will consider states that satisfy the following property (see figure
1
for the support of the automorphisms):

Assumption 3.2
.

Let
\omega\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
be

1.

such that there exists an automorphism
\alpha\in\textrm{QAut}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
and a product state

satisfying

\displaystyle\omega=\omega_{0}\circ\alpha.

(3.2)

2.

such that there exists a
\theta\in]0,\pi/2[
for which there exists a map,
\tilde{\beta}:G\rightarrow\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right):g\mapsto\tilde{\beta}_{g}
, satisfying

\displaystyle\omega\circ\tilde{\beta}_{g}

\displaystyle=\omega

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}_{g}=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{g,2}}}\right)\circ\eta^{L}_{g}\otimes\eta^{R}_{g}\circ\beta^{U}_{g}

(3.3)

for some
V_{g,2}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}),\eta^{L}_{g}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\nu^{-1}(C_{\theta}\cap L)}}}\right)
and
\eta^{R}_{g}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\nu(C_{\theta}\cap R)}}}\right)
.

3.

translation invariant (
\omega\circ\tau=\omega
).

We will also require a similar subclass of automorphisms and states on the quasi-local
C^{*}
-algebra over
\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathcal{A}_{L_{0}}
. We now define the assumption

Assumption 3.3
.

Take
\phi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A}_{L_{0}})
such that

1.

\phi\circ\beta_{g}=\phi
.

2.

there exist automorphisms
\tilde{\alpha}_{L}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{L_{0}\cap L}}}\right)
,
\tilde{\alpha}_{R}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{L_{0}\cap R}}}\right)
and a summable operator

such that

\phi_{0}=\phi\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b}}\right)\circ\tilde{\alpha}_{L}\otimes\tilde{\alpha}_{R}

(3.4)

is a product state.

We now state the result that using assumption
3.2
we can define an
H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})
-valued index.

Theorem 3.4
.

For any
\mathcal{A}
satisfying the construction from section
2.1
and any choice of on-site group action
U\in\hom(G,U(\mathbb{C}^{d}))
, there exists a map

\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}:\{\omega\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})|\omega\text{ satisfies assumption \ref{assumption}}\}\rightarrow H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})

(3.5)

that is well-defined (doesn’t depend on any of the choices in assumption
3.2
). This map will be consistent with conjecture
1.2
by which we mean that:

1.

For any
G
invariant and translation invariant family of interactions
\Phi\in\mathcal{B}_{F_{\phi}}([0,1])
(for some
0<\phi<1
) we have that
\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\omega)=\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\omega\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1})
.

2.

For
\phi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A}_{L_{0}})
, any one dimensional
G
-invariant state satisfying assumption
3.3
, the state
\omega_{\phi}
as defined in definition
2.1
satisfies
\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A}_{L_{0}},U}_{1d}(\phi)=\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\omega)
.

3.

This index is a group homomorphism under the stacking operator. By this, we mean that

\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A}^{2},U\otimes U}(\omega_{1}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\omega_{2})=\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\omega_{1})+\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\omega_{2}).

(3.6)

4.

The index is independent on the choice of basis for the on-site Hilbert space by which we mean that for any on-site unitary
V
we get that
\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V^{\dagger}}}\right)(U)}(\omega\circ i_{V})=\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\omega)
.

Proof.

This statement is proven in section
13
.
∎

The assumption from which we can define the
H^{1}(G,\mathbb{T})
-valued index is the same one with an additional translation invariance:

Assumption 3.5
.

Let
\omega\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
satisfy assumption
3.2
with the additional property that it is also translation invariant in the other direction (
\omega\circ\nu=\omega
).

Similar to before, we also require an assumption for states on the quasi-local
C^{*}
-algebra over
\mathbb{Z}
.

Assumption 3.6
.

Let
\phi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A}_{L_{0}})
satisfy assumption
3.3
with the additional property that
\phi
is translation invariant (
\phi\circ\nu=\phi
).

We are now ready to state the second main result:

Theorem 3.7
.

For any
\mathcal{A}
satisfying the construction from section
2.1
and any choice of on-site group action
U\in\hom(G,U(\mathbb{C}^{d}))
, there exists a map

\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}_{2\text{ trans}}:\{\omega\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})|\omega\text{ satisfies assumption \ref{assumption:2Translations}}\}\rightarrow H^{1}(G,\mathbb{T})

(3.7)

that is well-defined (doesn’t depend on any of the choices made in assumption
3.5
). This map will be consistent with conjecture
1.3
by which we mean the following:

1.

For any
G
invariant family of interactions that is translation invariant in both directions
\Phi\in\mathcal{B}_{F_{\phi}}([0,1])
we have that
\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}_{2\text{ trans}}(\omega)=\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}_{2\text{ trans}}(\omega\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1})
.

2.

For
\phi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A}_{L_{0}})
, any one dimensional
G
-invariant and translation invariant state satisfying assumption
3.3
, the state
\omega_{\phi}
as defined in
2.1
, satisfies
\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A}_{L_{0}},U}_{1d\text{ trans}}(\phi)=\text{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}_{2\text{ trans}}(\omega_{\phi})
.

3.

This index is a group homomorphism under the stacking operator by which we mean that

\textrm{Index}_{\text{2 trans}}^{\mathcal{A}^{2},U\otimes U}(\omega_{1}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\omega_{2})=\textrm{Index}_{\text{2 trans}}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\omega_{1})+\textrm{Index}_{\text{2 trans}}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\omega_{2}).

(3.8)

4.

The index is independent of the choice of basis for the on-site Hilbert space. By this we mean that, for any on-site unitary
V
, we get that
\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V^{\dagger}}}\right)(U)}_{\text{2 trans}}(\omega\circ i_{V})=\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}_{\text{2 trans}}(\omega)
.

Proof.

This statement is also proven in subsection
13
.
∎

3.2
SRE implies previous assumptions

The assumptions in the previous subsection are rather technical. However, it turns out that any SRE state, with some additional symmetries, naturally satisfies these assumptions. The precise statements and proofs are formulated in section
14
but we will describe them in words here.

As is shown in appendix
B
, which is based on the work in
[
10
]
, for any one parameter family of interactions
\Phi\in\mathcal{B}_{F_{\phi}}([0,1])
, we have that
\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}\in\textrm{QAut}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
. This implies the following. Take any SRE state
\omega
(see definition
2.2
), with entangler
\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}
, and product state
\omega_{0}
such that
\omega=\omega_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}
. We can simply define the
\alpha
from assumption
3.2
to be equal to this
\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}
. Similarly, in
[
10
]
it has been shown that if this state has an additional on-site symmetry, we can use the
\Phi
and
\omega_{0}
to construct some
\tilde{\beta}_{g}
that can be decomposed like in equation (
3.3
) for any angle.

It should also be said that we use the same definition of SRE (with the same
F
-function) for states over the quasi-local
C^{*}
-algebra over
\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathcal{A}_{L_{0}}
. This is because we will use the 1D SRE states to create translation-invariant 2D SRE states. Since the
F
-norm of a sum of disjoint interactions has an
F
-norm given by the highest of the
F
-norms of its sub-interactions, this means that to ensure that the resulting state has a well-defined index, we will need a similar
F
-norm and not something weaker.

Additionally, it turns out that the indices that are defined in the last subsection are independent of the choice of representative of the equivalence classes introduced in
2.4
.

All in all, we will show the following theorem:

Theorem 3.8
.

Take
(\omega,U)\in S_{0}
(see subsection
2.4
) for the algebra
\mathcal{A}
. The following statements now hold:

1.

\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}_{\text{no trans}}(\omega)
is well defined and independent on the choice of representative of
\expectationvalue{\omega}_{\sim}
.

2.

If additionally,
(\omega,U)\in S_{1}
,
\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\omega)
is also well defined and independent on the choice of representative of
\expectationvalue{\omega}_{\sim}^{1}
.

3.

If instead,
(\omega,U)\in S_{2}
,
\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\omega\circ\mu)
is well defined and independent on the choice of representative of
\expectationvalue{\omega}_{\sim}^{2}
.

4.

If additionally,
(\omega,U)\in S_{3}
,
\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}_{\textrm{2 trans}}(\omega)=\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}_{\textrm{2 trans}}(\omega\circ\mu)
is well defined and independent on the choice of representative of
\expectationvalue{\omega}_{\sim}^{3}
.

Proof.

This is shown in section
14
.
∎

3.3
Comparison with
[
10
]

The first thing we would like to remark on is that in
[
10
]
, a different equivalence class on states is used. First of all the set of states considered is a priori different. Namely, for any state
\psi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
, we define two different conditions

1.

There exists a
\Phi\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{SL}}
15
15

15

This is defined in
[
10
]
but it essentially means bounded interaction that is connected to a trivial (without overlapping terms) interaction.
such that
\psi
is the unique gapped groundstate of
\Phi
.

2.

\psi
is an SRE state.

However, in theorem 5.1 of
[
10
]
it is proven that the first item implies the second item. If one then looks at the definition of the index there, one could as well have started from the second condition. This is precisely what we did in this paper. As a side note, one should also be able to prove that the second item implies the first item by using theorem D.5 of
[
10
]
with
\mathcal{K}_{t}
the disentangler and
\Phi
a gapped interaction that has the product state as its ground state. However, we won’t work this out explicitly in this paper.

When we add the group, the story changes considerably. Let us define the two different equivalence classes. To this end, let
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
be two (pure)
G
-invariant states that are unique gapped groundstates of
\Phi_{0}
and
\Phi_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{SL},\beta}
respectively. The following two conditions define different equivalence classes:

1.

There exists a
G
-invariant, bounded path of gapped interactions
\lambda\mapsto\tilde{\Phi}(\lambda)
(see
[
10
]
for precise definitions) such that
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
are unique gapped groundstates of
\tilde{\Phi}(0)
and
\tilde{\Phi}(1)
respectively.

2.

There exists a
G
-invariant LGA,
\gamma^{\Psi}_{0;1}
such that
\psi_{1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}=\psi_{2}
.

A priori those two conditions are not equivalent. However, in
[
10
]
it is proven that the first equivalence implies the second one. The proof that equivalent states have the same index only uses the second condition. This means that one could also have taken the second equivalence condition as the starting point. This is exactly what we do in this paper. One should also be able to show that the second condition implies the first one using a similar construction as in the above remark without the group and averaging the resulting interaction over the group (where one then has to use that a finite group has a normalized Haar measure).

There were two things that are in theorems
3.4
and
3.7
but were not discussed in
[
10
]
. This is the part that proves that the index is a group homomorphism under stacking and the part that the index is independent of the choice of basis for the on-site Hilbert space. However, on inspection of the arguments used in this paper one can conclude that these also hold for the
H^{3}
-valued index.

All in all, we have provided a proof that the work done in
[
10
]
also implies the following theorem:

Theorem 3.9
.

For any
\mathcal{A}
satisfying the construction from section
2.1
and any choice of on-site group action
U\in\hom(G,U(\mathbb{C}^{d}))
, there exists a map

\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}_{\text{no trans}}:\{\omega\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})|\omega\text{ is SRE and }G\text{-invariant}\}\rightarrow H^{3}(G,\mathbb{T})

(3.9)

that is well-defined (doesn’t depend on the choice of disentangler). This map will satisfy:

1.

For any
G
invariant family of interactions
\Phi\in\mathcal{B}_{F_{\phi}}([0,1])
we have that

\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}_{\text{no trans}}(\omega)=\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}_{\text{no trans}}(\omega\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}).

(3.10)

2.

This index is a group homomorphism under the stacking operator by which we mean that

\textrm{Index}_{\text{no trans}}^{\mathcal{A}^{2},U\otimes U}(\omega_{1}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\omega_{2})=\textrm{Index}_{\text{no trans}}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\omega_{1})+\textrm{Index}_{\text{no trans}}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\omega_{2}).

(3.11)

3.

The index is independent of the choice of basis for the on-site Hilbert space by which we mean that for any on-site unitary
V
we get that

\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V^{\dagger}}}\right)(U)}_{\text{no trans}}(\omega\circ i_{V})=\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}_{\text{no trans}}(\omega).

(3.12)

4
Translation invariance in one dimension

Let
\mathcal{A}_{L_{0}}
be the quasi-local
C^{*}
algebra defined on the one-dimensional lattice. Let
U_{i}\in\hom(G,\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{i}))
(for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
) be the on-site group action. Let
\beta_{g}
be again so that for each finite
I\subset\mathbb{Z}
,
\beta_{g}^{I}=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bigotimes_{i\in I}U_{i}(g)}}\right)
. In this section, we will look at states satisfying the split property. We say that
\omega\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A}_{L_{0}})
satisfies the split property iff. it is quasi equivalent to
\omega|_{L}\otimes\omega|_{R}
. This implies that there exists a GNS triple of
\omega
,
(\mathcal{H},\pi,\Omega)
that is of the form
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{L}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{R}
,
\pi=\pi_{L}\otimes\pi_{R}
. We will denote the space of pure states satisfying the split property by
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
. Now let us additionally ask that
\omega
is invariant under the group action (
\omega\circ\beta_{g}=\omega
). The following now holds:

Lemma 4.1
.

There exist maps
u_{\sigma}:G\rightarrow\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{\sigma})
(for all
\sigma\in\{L,R\}
) satisfying

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u_{\sigma}(g)}}\right)\circ\pi_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\sigma}\circ\beta_{g}^{\sigma}

\displaystyle u_{L}(g)\otimes u_{R}(g)\Omega

\displaystyle=\Omega.

(4.1)

They are unique up to a
G
-dependent phase. Furthermore, there exists a 2-cochain
C:G^{2}\rightarrow U(1)
that satisfies

\displaystyle u_{R}(g)u_{R}(h)u_{R}(gh)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=C(g,h)\mathbbm{1}_{H_{R}}

\displaystyle u_{L}(g)u_{L}(h)u_{L}(gh)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=C(g,h)^{-1}\mathbbm{1}_{H_{L}}.

(4.2)

Its second group cohomology class is independent of the choice of GNS triple or the choice of (a
G
-dependent) phase in
u_{R}(g)
. This index is invariant under locally generated automorphisms generated by
G
-invariant interactions.

Proof.

See
[
9
]
.
∎

The cohomology class of this 2-cochain is called the one-dimensional SPT index. Now let us assume additionally (on top of the split property and the
G
-invariance) that
\omega
be translation invariant. To this end, let
\nu
be the automorphism that translates
\mathcal{B}
by one site to the right. We assume that
\omega\circ\nu=\omega
. The following now holds:

Lemma 4.2
.

There exists a unique
w\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H})
such that

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)\circ\pi

\displaystyle=\pi\circ\nu

\displaystyle w\Omega

\displaystyle=\Omega.

(4.3)

Proof.

Since
\omega\circ\nu=\omega
, we get that
(\mathcal{H},\pi\circ\nu,\Omega)\underset{\text{u.e.}}{\sim}(\mathcal{H},\pi,\Omega)
. The uniqueness of the GNS triple now implies the existence of this
w
. Its uniqueness follows from the irreducibility of
\pi
.
∎

We can use this to define an additional 1d index for translation invariant states. To do this, we will need to use the finiteness of the group explicitly. We will use one particular property of this, namely that finite groups have discrete U(1) representations. By this we mean the following:

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
G
be a finite group. Let
\alpha:[0,1]\rightarrow\hom(G,U(1)):\lambda\mapsto\alpha_{\lambda}
be a map such that
\lambda\mapsto\alpha_{\lambda}(g)
is continuous for all
g\in G
. If
\alpha_{0}(g)=1
for all
g\in G
then
\alpha_{\lambda}(g)=1
for all
g\in G
and all
\lambda\in[0,1]
.

Proof.

Take
\alpha
as above with
\alpha_{0}(g)=1
for all
g\in G
, we will prove that
\alpha_{\lambda}(g)=1
for all
g\in G
and all
\lambda\in[0,1]
. To do this, let
n
be the order of
G
. It follows from Lagrange’s theorem that
g^{n}=\mathbbm{1}_{G}
for all
g\in G
. This implies that there exists a unique (
\lambda
and
g
dependent)
k\in\mathbb{N}\cap[0,n-1]
such that
\alpha_{\lambda}(g)=\exp(2\pi ik/n)
. Since for any fixed
g\in G
,
\alpha_{\lambda}(g)
has to be continuously connected to
1
, we deduce that
k=0
and hence the result follows.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

There exists a (unique)
\alpha\in\hom(G,U(1))
satisfying that

\pi(U_{-1}(g))(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{L}}\otimes u_{R}(g))=\alpha(g)w^{\dagger}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{L}}\otimes u_{R}(g))w.

(4.4)

This index is invariant under locally generated automorphisms generated by one-parameter families of interactions in
\mathcal{B}_{F_{\phi}}([0,1])
(with
F_{\phi}
as presented in (
2.13
)) that are both
G
and translation invariant.

Proof.

The fact that
\alpha
exists follows from the fact that
\pi
is irreducible and combined with the fact that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi(U_{-1}(g))(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{L}}\otimes u_{R}(g))}}\right)\circ\pi=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w^{\dagger}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{L}}\otimes u_{R}(g))w}}\right)\circ\pi=\pi\circ\beta_{g}^{[-1,\infty[}.

(4.5)

The fact that
\alpha
is a
U(1)-
representation follows from the fact that
u_{R}
is the lift of a projective representation. Suppose that
u_{R}(g)u_{R}(h)=C(g,h)u_{R}(gh)
then

\pi(U_{-1}(g))(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{L}}\otimes u_{R}(g))\pi(U_{-1}(h))(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{L}}\otimes u_{R}(h))=C(g,h)\pi(U_{-1}(gh))(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{L}}\otimes u_{R}(gh))

(4.6)

on the one hand whereas

w^{\dagger}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{L}}\otimes u_{R}(g))ww^{\dagger}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{L}}\otimes u_{R}(h))w=C(g,h)w^{\dagger}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{L}}\otimes u_{R}(gh))w.

(4.7)

These two equations can only be consistent if
\alpha(g)\alpha(h)=\alpha(gh)
. The proof that this is independent of the choice of GNS triple is straightforward. Now we need to show that this index is invariant under LGA’s generated by
G
-invariant interactions. To this end let
F_{\phi}
be our F-function from equation (
2.13
). Let
\Phi\in\mathcal{B}_{F_{\phi}}([0,1])
be a
G
-invariant, translation invariant interaction. For simplicity of notation let
\Phi_{\text{split}}=\Phi_{\nu^{-1}(L)}+\Phi_{\nu(R)}
. Let
\lambda\in[0,1]\mapsto A_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
be a norm continuous one-parameter family such that

\displaystyle\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;\lambda}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{\lambda}}}\right)\circ\gamma^{\Phi_{\text{split}}}_{0;\lambda}

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}}.

(4.8)

and define
A\vcentcolon=A_{1}
. This exists because of lemma
A.1
. By construction
(\mathcal{H},\pi\circ\gamma^{\Phi_{\text{split}}}_{0;1},\pi(A^{\dagger})\Omega)
is a GNS triple of
\omega\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}
. Let
\tilde{w}\vcentcolon=\pi(A^{\dagger})w\pi(A)
then
\tilde{\alpha}(g)\vcentcolon=\pi(U_{-1}(g))u_{R}(g)\tilde{w}^{\dagger}u_{R}(g)^{\dagger}\tilde{w}
is the index of
\omega\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}
. Putting the
A
’s to the front and inserting the old index gives

\tilde{\alpha}(g)=\alpha(g)w^{\dagger}\pi\circ\beta_{g}^{R}(\nu(A^{\dagger})A)\pi(A^{\dagger}\nu(A))w.

(4.9)

This shows that we conclude the proof if we can show that
\beta_{g}^{R}(\nu(A^{\dagger})A)=\nu(A^{\dagger})A
. We will do this proof in two steps. The first step is that we prove that
\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta_{g}^{R}(\nu(A^{\dagger})A)}}\right)=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\nu(A^{\dagger})A}}\right)
. We use the first equation of (
4.8
) and obtain

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta_{g}^{R}(\nu(A^{\dagger})A)}}\right)=\text{\sout{$\beta_{g}^{R}$}}\circ\gamma^{\nu(\Phi_{\text{split}})}_{0;1}\circ\text{\sout{$\nu\circ\nu^{-1}$}}\circ\gamma^{\Phi_{\text{split}}}_{1;0}\circ\text{\sout{$(\beta_{g}^{R})^{-1}$}}=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\nu(A^{\dagger})A}}\right).

(4.10)

This shows that there exists some
\alpha^{\prime}\in\hom(G,U(1))
such that
\alpha^{\prime}(g)=\beta_{g}^{R}(\nu(A^{\dagger})A)(A^{\dagger}\nu(A))
. However since this has to hold for each
\lambda\in[0,1]
, we can define for each
\lambda
some
\alpha_{\lambda}\in\hom(G,U(1))
through
\alpha_{\lambda}(g)=\beta_{g}^{R}(\nu(A^{\dagger}_{\lambda})A_{\lambda})(A^{\dagger}_{\lambda}\nu(A_{\lambda}))
. This implies that
\alpha^{\prime}(g)=\alpha_{1}(g)
has to be connected to
\alpha_{0}(g)=1
. Now we will use that the
U(1)
representations of a finite group are discrete. More specifically we will use lemma
4.3
. This implies that
\alpha^{\prime}(g)=1
for all
g\in G
.
∎

All together we constructed a proof of the following theorem:

Theorem 4.5
.

There exist maps

\displaystyle\textrm{Index}_{1d}

\displaystyle:\{\omega\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})|\omega\circ\beta_{g}=\omega\}\rightarrow H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})

(4.11)

\displaystyle\textrm{Index}_{1d\text{ trans}}

\displaystyle:\{\omega\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})|\omega\circ\beta_{g}=\omega\text{ and }\omega\circ\nu=\omega\}\rightarrow H^{1}(G,\mathbb{T})\cong\hom(G,\mathbb{T}).

For any
G-
invariant family of interactions
\Phi\in\mathcal{B}_{F}([0,1])
, we have that
\textrm{Index}_{1d}(\omega)=\textrm{Index}_{1d}(\omega\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1})
. For any
G-
invariant family of interactions
\Phi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{F}([0,1])
that is also translation invariant, we have that
\textrm{Index}_{1d\text{ trans}}(\omega)=\textrm{Index}_{1d\text{ trans}}(\omega\circ\gamma^{\Phi^{\prime}}_{0;1})
.

5
Cone and translation operators

\theta
\xi_{L}
\xi_{R}
\Theta

\theta
\xi_{L}
\xi_{R}
\Theta

\theta
\xi_{L}
\xi_{R}
\Theta

\theta
\xi_{L}
\xi_{R}
\Theta

Figure 2
:
This is a graphical depiction of the support of the automorphisms
\xi_{L}
and
\xi_{R}
with respect to
\Theta
in the definitions of
\textrm{Cone}_{\sigma}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
,
\textrm{Cone}_{\sigma}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
,
\textrm{Cone}_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
and
\textrm{Cone}_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
respectively.

In this section, we will fix certain objects that we will later on use to define an
H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})-
valued index for states satisfying assumption
3.2
and an
H^{1}(G,\mathbb{T})-
valued index for states satisfying assumption
3.5
. To this end, let
\omega\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
satisfy assumption
3.2
. Fix a product state
\omega_{0}
and an
\alpha\in\textrm{QAut}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
such that
\omega=\omega_{0}\circ\alpha
. Fix
(\mathcal{H}_{0}=\mathcal{H}_{L}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{R},\pi_{0}=\pi_{L}\otimes\pi_{R},\Omega_{0})
, a GNS triple (see theorem 2.3.16 in
[
2
]
) of
\omega_{0}
where (
\forall\sigma\in\{L,R\}
)
\pi_{\sigma}:\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\sigma})
is a GNS representation of the restriction of
\omega_{0}
to
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}
. We also fix,
V_{1}
,
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{L}\otimes\alpha_{R}
and
\Theta
as a choice of decomposition for
\alpha
such that
\Theta
and
\eta_{g}
commute (see figure
1
). Because we will be working with states that are translation invariant, it makes sense to define the translation action on the GNS space of
\omega_{0}
:

Lemma 5.1
.

There exists a unique
v\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
such that

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\tau\circ\Theta^{-1}\circ\alpha_{0}^{-1}

and

\displaystyle\pi_{0}(V_{1})v\pi_{0}(V_{1}^{\dagger})\Omega_{0}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{0}.

(5.1)

If additionally,
\omega
satisfies assumption
3.5
, there exists a unique
w\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
such that

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\nu\circ\Theta^{-1}\circ\alpha_{0}^{-1}

and

\displaystyle\pi_{0}(V_{1})w\pi_{0}(V_{1}^{\dagger})\Omega_{0}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{0}.

(5.2)

Proof.

We only do the first part as the second part is analogous. Since
\omega\circ\tau=\omega
we get by the uniqueness of the GNS triple (see Corollary 2.3.17 and Theorem 2.3.19 from
[
2
]
) that there exists a unique
\tilde{v}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
satisfying

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{v}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha\circ\tau

\displaystyle\tilde{v}\Omega_{0}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{0}.

(5.3)

Since
\alpha=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{1}}}\right)\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta
we get that

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi_{0}(V_{1}^{\dagger})\tilde{v}\pi_{0}(V_{1})}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\tau

\displaystyle\tilde{v}\Omega_{0}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{0}.

(5.4)

Choosing
v=\pi_{0}(V_{1}^{\dagger})\tilde{v}\pi_{0}(V_{1})
concludes the proof.
∎

We now define a subgroup of
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
that includes the representations of cone automorphisms (see figure
2
for a graphical depiction of the supports of
\xi
):

Definition 5.2
.

Take
0<\theta<\pi/2
,
\alpha_{L}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{L}}}\right)
,
\alpha_{R}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{R}}}\right)
and
\alpha_{0}\vcentcolon=\alpha_{L}\otimes\alpha_{R}
. Take
\sigma\in\{L,R\}
and
x\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{\sigma})\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}/\sigma}}
then we say that
x
is a cone operator on
\sigma
(or in short
x\in\textrm{Cone}_{\sigma}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
) if and only if there exists a
\xi\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{W(C_{\theta})\cap\sigma}}}\right)
such that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\xi.

(5.5)

\textrm{Cone}_{\sigma}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
is a subgroup of
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{\sigma})\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}/\sigma}
. We have that

\displaystyle\textrm{Cone}_{R}(\alpha_{0},\theta)

\displaystyle\subseteq\textrm{Cone}_{L}(\alpha_{0},\theta)^{\prime}

\displaystyle\textrm{Cone}_{L}(\alpha_{0},\theta)

\displaystyle\subseteq\textrm{Cone}_{R}(\alpha_{0},\theta)^{\prime}.

(5.6)

If additionally
\xi\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{C_{\theta}\cap\sigma}}}\right)
,
\xi\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\nu^{\sigma}(W(C_{\theta})\cap\sigma)}}}\right)
or
\xi\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\nu^{\sigma}(C_{\theta}\cap\sigma)}}}\right)
respectively, we will say that
x\in\textrm{Cone}_{\sigma}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
,
x\in\textrm{Cone}_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
or
x\in\textrm{Cone}_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
respectively.

Proof.

The proof of equation (
5.6
) just follows from the fact that we defined
\textrm{Cone}_{\sigma}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
such that

\displaystyle\textrm{Cone}_{L}(\alpha_{0},\theta)

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{L})\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{R}}

\displaystyle\textrm{Cone}_{R}(\alpha_{0},\theta)

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{L}}\otimes\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{R}).

(5.7)

∎

We will also define a generalization of this. We will define a subgroup of
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
that includes both the group
\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{R}))
and the representations of cone automorphisms:

Definition 5.3
.

Take
0<\theta<\pi/2
,
\alpha_{L}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{L}}}\right)
,
\alpha_{R}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{R}}}\right)
and
\Theta\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{W(C_{\theta})^{c}}}}\right)
. Take
\sigma\in\{L,R\}
and take
x\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
then we say that
x
is an inner after cone operator on
\sigma
(or in short
x\in\textrm{IAC}_{\sigma}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)
) if there exists an
A\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma})
and a
y\in\textrm{Cone}_{\sigma}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
such that

x=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(A)y.

(5.8)

\textrm{IAC}_{\sigma}
is a subgroup of
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
. We get that

\displaystyle\textrm{IAC}_{R}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)

\displaystyle\subseteq\textrm{IAC}_{L}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)^{\prime}

\displaystyle\textrm{IAC}_{L}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)

\displaystyle\subseteq\textrm{IAC}_{R}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)^{\prime}.

(5.9)

If, additionally,
y\in\textrm{Cone}_{\sigma}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
we will say that
x\in\textrm{IAC}_{\sigma}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)
. If
A\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)})
and
y\in\textrm{Cone}_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
, we say that
x\in\textrm{IAC}_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)
. If
A\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)})
and
y\in\textrm{Cone}_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
, we say that
x\in\textrm{IAC}_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)
.

Proof.

Take
u_{L}\in\textrm{IAC}_{L}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)
and
u_{R}\in\textrm{IAC}_{R}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)
arbitrary. Take
A_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma})
and
v_{\sigma}\in\textrm{Cone}_{\sigma}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
(for
\sigma\in\{L,R\}
) such that
u_{\sigma}=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(A_{\sigma})v_{\sigma}
. We have to prove that
[u_{L},u_{R}]=0
. To do this, we need three things. First of all, by construction, we get that
[A_{L},A_{R}]=0
. Secondly, we showed in
5.6
that
[v_{L},v_{R}]=0
. We now only have to show that
[\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(A_{L}),v_{R}]
(and vice versa) as using these three properties repeatedly on the expression
[u_{L},u_{R}]
shows that it vanishes. This property is clearly equivalent to showing that
\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{R}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(A_{L})=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(A_{L})
. Using definition
5.2
and the fact that
\xi_{R}
and
\Theta
commute, this reduces to showing that
\xi_{R}(A_{L})=A_{L}
. This is true by construction.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

The main reason for the introduction of the
\textrm{Cone}_{\sigma}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
and
\textrm{IAC}^{W}_{\sigma}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)
subgroups is that they are still (inner after) cone operators when translated. More specifically, let
y\in\textrm{Cone}_{\sigma}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
then both
vyv^{\dagger}
and
v^{\dagger}yv
are elements of
\textrm{Cone}_{\sigma}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
. The analogous statement is also true for the
IAC
operators. Similarly, in the case where there are two translation symmetries, we get that for
x\in\textrm{IAC}_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
or
x\in\textrm{IAC}_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)
, both
wxw^{\dagger}
and
w^{\dagger}xw
are in
\textrm{IAC}_{\sigma}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)
or
\textrm{IAC}_{\sigma}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)
respectively.

There is a standard lemma we will often refer to:

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
be arbitrary unital
C^{*}
-algebras. Let
(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}},\pi_{\mathcal{A}})
and
(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}},\pi_{\mathcal{B}})
be arbitrary irreducible
*
-representations on
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
respectively. Let
U\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}})
be such that there exists an
\alpha_{\mathcal{A}}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
and an
\alpha_{\mathcal{B}}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{B}}}\right)
satisfying

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(U}}\right)\circ(\pi_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\pi_{\mathcal{B}})=\pi_{\mathcal{A}}\circ\alpha_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\pi_{\mathcal{B}}\circ\alpha_{\mathcal{B}}

(5.10)

then there exists a
U_{\mathcal{A}}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})
and a
U_{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}})
such that
U=U_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes U_{\mathcal{B}}
.

Proof.

From the assumptions we see that
\forall A\in\mathcal{A}

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(U}}\right)\circ\pi_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(A\otimes\mathbbm{1})=\pi_{\mathcal{A}}\circ\alpha_{\mathcal{A}}(A)\otimes\mathbbm{1}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})\otimes\mathbbm{1}.

(5.11)

Since
\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(U}}\right)
is continuous in weak operator topology (on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}})
), it follows that we can extend the map

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(U}}\right)|_{\textrm{Im}(\pi_{\mathcal{A}})}:\textrm{Im}(\pi_{\mathcal{A}})\otimes\mathbbm{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})\otimes\mathbbm{1}

(5.12)

to the closure in weak operator topology. By irreducibility (and the Von Neumann bicommutant theorem), we get that
\pi_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{A})^{\prime\prime}=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})
and therefore we get a map

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(U}}\right)|_{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})\otimes\mathbbm{1}}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})\otimes\mathbbm{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})\otimes\mathbbm{1}.

(5.13)

By restriction, this gives rise to an automorphism
\tau_{\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})
. By Wigners theorem any automorphism of
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})
is inner and therefore there exists a
U_{\mathcal{A}}
such that
\tau_{\mathcal{A}}=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(U_{\mathcal{A}}}}\right)
. Doing the same on
\mathcal{B}
gives us similarly a
\tau_{\mathcal{B}}
and a
U_{\mathcal{B}}
. We now get

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(U}}\right)\circ(\pi_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\pi_{\mathcal{B}})=\tau_{\mathcal{A}}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\tau_{\mathcal{B}}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{B}}=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(U_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes U_{\mathcal{B}}}}\right)\circ(\pi_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\pi_{\mathcal{B}}).

(5.14)

By the irreducibility of
\pi_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\pi_{\mathcal{B}}
we get that (up to some irrelevant phase) we need that
U=U_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes U_{\mathcal{B}}
concluding the proof.
∎

Among other things, it shows that if a unitary on the GNS space of
\omega_{0}
has an adjoint action that is the product of cones then it is a product of cone operators:

Lemma 5.6
.

Take
x\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
such that there exist
\xi_{\sigma}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{C_{\theta}\cap\sigma}}}\right)
(for
\sigma\in\{L,R\}
) satisfying

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\xi_{L}\otimes\xi_{R}

(5.15)

then there exist
x_{\sigma}\in\textrm{Cone}_{\sigma}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
such that
x=x_{L}\otimes x_{R}
. These are unique up to a phase.

Proof.

Using lemma
5.5
, the result follows.
∎

Following Ogata
[
10
]
, we now define objects using the following lemma:

Lemma 5.7
.

There unitaries
W_{g}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
and
u_{\sigma}(g,h)\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{\sigma})
for all
g,h\in G
and for all
\sigma\in\{L,R\}
satisfying

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\eta_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}\circ\Theta^{-1}\circ\alpha_{0}^{-1}

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u_{\sigma}(g,h)}}\right)\circ\pi_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\sigma}\circ\alpha_{\sigma}\circ\eta_{g}^{\sigma}\circ\beta_{g}^{\sigma U}\circ\eta_{h}^{\sigma}\circ(\beta_{g}^{\sigma U})^{-1}\circ(\eta^{\sigma}_{gh})^{-1}\circ\alpha_{\sigma}^{-1}

(5.16)

\displaystyle u_{L}(g,h)\otimes u_{R}(g,h)

\displaystyle=W_{g}W_{h}W_{gh}^{-1}.

Proof.

The result follows from the fact that assumption
3.2
is sufficient to define the objects used in lemma 2.1 and definition 2.2 of
[
10
]
.
∎

These objects will be the starting point for our definition. Clearly the
u_{\sigma}(g,h)
are elements of
\textrm{Cone}_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
. The
W_{g}
have the following property:

Lemma 5.8
.

Take
\Xi^{\sigma}\in\textrm{Cone}_{\sigma}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
(for all
\sigma\in\{L,R\}
), then
\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)(\Xi^{\sigma})\in\textrm{Cone}_{\sigma}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
(for all
g\in G
). If, more generally, we take
\Xi^{\sigma}\in\textrm{IAC}_{\sigma}(\alpha_{0},\Theta)
, then
\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)(\Xi^{\sigma})\in\textrm{IAC}_{\sigma}(\alpha_{0},\Theta)
. Similarly if
\Xi^{\sigma}\in\textrm{Cone}_{\sigma}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
, then
\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)(\Xi^{\sigma})\in\textrm{Cone}_{\sigma}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
. If additionally,
\Xi^{\sigma}\in\textrm{Cone}_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
or
\Xi^{\sigma}\in\textrm{Cone}_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
,
\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)(\Xi^{\sigma})\in\textrm{Cone}_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
and
\textrm{Cone}_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
respectively.

Proof.

Take
\tilde{\Xi}^{\sigma}
such that
\Xi^{\sigma}=\tilde{\Xi}^{\sigma}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\sigma^{c}}}
. Take
\xi_{\sigma}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{W(C_{\theta})\cap\sigma}}}\right)
such that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{\Xi}^{\sigma}}}\right)\circ\pi_{\sigma}\circ\alpha_{\sigma}=\pi_{\sigma}\circ\alpha_{\sigma}\circ\xi_{\sigma}.

(5.17)

We have that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)(\Xi^{R})}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\zeta_{g,R}

(5.18)

where

\zeta_{g,\sigma}\vcentcolon=\eta_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}\circ\xi_{\sigma}\circ(\beta_{g}^{U})^{-1}\circ\eta_{g}^{-1}.

(5.19)

Since
\zeta_{g,\sigma}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{C_{\theta}}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}}}\right)
we get that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)(\Xi^{R})}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}=\pi_{L}\circ\alpha_{L}\otimes\pi_{R}\circ\alpha_{R}\circ\zeta_{g,R}.

(5.20)

Using lemma
5.6
there exists a
Z_{g,R}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{R})
satisfying that
\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)(\Xi^{R})=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{L}}\otimes Z_{g,R}
. To show the second result we only need to observe that for any
A\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{R})
we get that

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(A)\Xi_{R}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(A)\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)(\Xi_{R})

(5.21)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\xi_{R}(A)\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)(\Xi_{R}).

By the previous result, this is clearly in
\textrm{IAC}_{R}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)
concluding the proof.
∎

We now have to define two more objects (remember that
v
was defined in lemma
5.1
):

Lemma 5.9
.

There exist
K_{g}^{L}\in\textrm{Cone}_{\nu^{L}(L)}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
and
K_{g}^{R}\in\textrm{Cone}_{\nu^{R}(R)}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
such that

v^{\dagger}W_{g}vW_{g}^{\dagger}=K_{g}^{L}\otimes K_{g}^{R}=K_{g}

(5.22)

and they are unique (up to a phase). They satisfy the identity

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K^{R}_{g}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\eta_{g}^{R}\circ\beta_{g}^{RU}\circ\tau\circ(\beta_{g}^{RU})^{-1}\circ(\eta_{g}^{R})^{-1}\circ\alpha_{0}^{-1}.\quad

(5.23)

Proof.

By some straightforward calculation, we have that

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K_{g}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v^{\dagger}W_{g}vW_{g}^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}

(5.24)

\displaystyle=\bigotimes_{\sigma=L,R}\pi_{\sigma}\circ\alpha_{\sigma}\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\eta_{g}^{\sigma}\circ\beta_{g}^{\sigma U}\circ\tau\circ(\beta_{g}^{\sigma U})^{-1}\circ(\eta_{g}^{\sigma})^{-1}\circ\alpha_{\sigma}^{-1}.

Using lemma
5.6
concludes the proof that these unitaries exist and are unique up to a
G-
dependent phase.
∎

If, additionally,
\omega
satisfies assumption
3.6
, we can use these objects to define
b^{\sigma}_{g}
:

Lemma 5.10
.

There exist a
b_{g}^{L}\in\textrm{Cone}_{\nu^{-1}(L)}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
and a
b_{g}^{R}\in\textrm{Cone}_{R}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
(both unique up to an exchange of a
G-
dependent phase) satisfying that

w^{\dagger}W_{g}wW_{g}^{\dagger}=b_{g}:=b_{g}^{L}\otimes b_{g}^{R}.

(5.25)

It will satisfy

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{g}^{\sigma}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}/\sigma}}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\eta_{g}^{\sigma}\circ\nu\circ(\eta_{g}^{\sigma})^{-1}.

(5.26)

Proof.

It is easy enough to show that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w^{\dagger}W_{g}wW_{g}^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}=\pi_{0}\circ(\alpha_{L}\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\eta_{g}^{L}\circ\nu\circ(\eta_{g}^{L})^{-1}\circ\alpha_{L}^{-1})\otimes(\alpha_{R}\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\eta_{g}^{R}\circ\nu\circ(\eta_{g}^{R})^{-1}\circ\alpha_{R}^{-1}).

(5.27)

To do this one only has to use that
\beta_{g}^{U}
commutes with
\nu
. By lemma
5.6
the result now follows.
∎

6
Defining the indices

In this section, we will use the objects defined in section
5
to define the
H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})
-valued index introduced in theorem
3.4
and the
H^{1}(G,\mathbb{T})
-valued index introduced in theorem
3.7
.

6.1
The
H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})
-valued index

Lemma 6.1
.

There exists a
C:G^{2}\rightarrow U(1)
such that

K_{g}^{R}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K_{h}^{R}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{h}W_{gh}^{\dagger}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((K_{gh}^{R})^{\dagger}}}\right)}}\right)u_{R}(g,h)=C(g,h)v^{\dagger}u_{R}(g,h)v

(6.1)

for all
g,h\in G.

Proof.

Since the GNS representation is irreducible this is equivalent to showing that the left and righthand side of equation (
6.1
) have the same adjoint action on the GNS representation. We first prove the result for the full tensor product. By using the definition of
u(g,h)
we get

\displaystyle v^{\dagger}u_{L}(g,h)vv^{\dagger}u_{R}(g,h)v

\displaystyle=v^{\dagger}(u_{L}(g,h)\otimes u_{R}(g,h))v=v^{\dagger}W_{g}W_{h}W_{gh}^{-1}v.

(6.2)

Now using the definition of
K_{g}
gives:

(
6.2
)

\displaystyle=K_{g}W_{g}v^{\dagger}W_{h}W_{gh}^{-1}v=K_{g}W_{g}K_{h}W_{h}v^{\dagger}W_{gh}^{-1}v

(6.3)

\displaystyle=K_{g}W_{g}K_{h}W_{h}W_{gh}^{\dagger}K_{gh}^{\dagger}=K_{g}W_{g}K_{h}W_{h}W_{gh}^{\dagger}K_{gh}^{\dagger}W_{gh}W_{h}^{\dagger}W_{g}^{\dagger}W_{g}W_{h}W_{gh}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=K_{g}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K_{h}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{h}W_{gh}^{\dagger}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K_{gh}^{\dagger}}}\right)}}\right)u_{L}(g,h)\otimes u_{R}(g,h).

Using lemma
5.8
we get that

\displaystyle(K_{g}^{L}\otimes K_{g}^{R})\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((K_{h}^{L}\otimes K_{h}^{R})\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{h}W_{gh}^{\dagger}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((K_{gh}^{L}\otimes K_{gh}^{R})^{-1}}}\right)}}\right)

(6.4)

\displaystyle=K_{g}^{L}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K_{h}^{L}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{h}W_{gh}^{\dagger}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((K_{gh}^{L})^{\dagger}}}\right)}}\right)\otimes K_{g}^{R}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K_{h}^{R}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{h}W_{gh}^{\dagger}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((K_{gh}^{R})^{\dagger}}}\right)}}\right)

concluding the proof.
∎

We the next goal is to prove that this function
C
is a
2
-cochain. We will do this by proving that the
3
-cochain constructed in
[
10
]
is invariant under some substitution. More precisely, we will use:

Lemma 6.2
.

The three-cochain
C^{\prime}(g,h,k)
defined through

u_{R}(g,h)u_{R}(gh,k)u_{R}(g,hk)^{\dagger}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)(u_{R}(h,k)^{\dagger})=C^{\prime}(g,h,k)\mathbbm{1}.

(6.5)

is invariant under the substitution

\displaystyle W_{g}

\displaystyle\rightarrow K_{g}W_{g}

\displaystyle u_{R}(g,h)

\displaystyle\rightarrow K_{g}^{R}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K_{h}^{R}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{h}W_{gh}^{\dagger}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((K_{gh}^{R})^{\dagger}}}\right)}}\right)u_{R}(g,h)

(6.6)

Proof.

Inserting substitution (
6.6
) into (
6.5
) gives

\displaystyle u_{R}(g,h)u_{R}(gh,k)u_{R}(g,hk)^{\dagger}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u_{R}(h,k)^{\dagger}}}\right)

(6.7)

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle K_{g}^{R}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K_{h}^{R}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{h}W_{gh}^{\dagger}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((K_{gh}^{R})^{\dagger}}}\right)}}\right)u_{R}(g,h)

(6.8)

\displaystyle K_{gh}^{R}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{gh}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K_{k}^{R}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{k}W_{ghk}^{\dagger}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((K_{ghk}^{R})^{\dagger}}}\right)}}\right)u_{R}(gh,k)

\displaystyle u_{R}(g,hk)^{\dagger}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{hk}W_{ghk}^{\dagger}}}\right)(K^{R}_{ghk})(K^{R}_{hk})^{\dagger}}}\right)(K^{R}_{g})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K_{g}W_{g}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u_{R}(h,k)^{\dagger}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{h}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{k}W_{hk}^{\dagger}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K_{hk}^{R}}}\right)(K^{R}_{k})^{\dagger}}}\right)(K^{R}_{h})^{\dagger}}}\right).

Using the fact that
W_{g}W_{h}W_{gh}^{\dagger}=u_{L}\otimes u_{R}(g,h)
and lemma
5.8
(for the substitution
K_{g}\rightarrow K_{g}^{R}
in the last line) one now gets

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{g}^{R}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K_{h}^{R}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{h}W_{gh}^{\dagger}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((K_{gh}^{R})^{\dagger}}}\right)}}\right)\underline{W_{g}W_{h}W_{gh}^{\dagger}u_{L}(g,h)^{\dagger}}

(6.9)
