\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\phi_{2}(w^{-p}W_{g}w^{p})}}\right)(\tilde{\phi}_{1}(x))

(9.30)

\displaystyle\phi_{2}(\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v^{s}}}\right)(w^{-p}W_{g}w^{p}))

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v^{s}}}\right)(\phi_{2}(w^{-p}W_{g}w^{p}))

(9.31)

If additionally
x\in\textrm{IAC}_{L\times R}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)
we get that

\displaystyle\phi_{1}(\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v^{s}}}\right)(x))

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v^{s}}}\right)(\phi_{1}(x)).

(9.32)

Proof.

Take
x\in\textrm{IAC}_{R}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)
arbitrary (the proof for
x\in\textrm{IAC}_{L}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)
is analogous). Take
A\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{R})
and
y\in\textrm{Cone}_{R}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
such that
x=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(A)y
and
\xi\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{C_{\theta}}\cap\mathcal{A}_{R}}}\right)
the automorphism such that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\xi.

(9.33)

Take
a
and
\Phi
as given in equation (
9.1
) and take
\tilde{a}=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(a)
. We now get

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w^{-p}W_{g}w^{p}}}\right)(x)

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w^{-p}W_{g}w^{p}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(A)\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w^{-p}W_{g}w^{p}}}\right)(y)

(9.34)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\nu^{-p}\circ\eta_{g}\circ\nu^{p}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}(A)\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w^{-p}W_{g}w^{p}}}\right)(y).

We still have that
\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w^{-p}W_{g}w^{p}}}\right)(y)\in\textrm{Cone}_{R}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
. This is because there exists a
\tilde{\xi}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{C_{\theta}}\cap\mathcal{A}_{R}}}\right)
such that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w^{-p}W_{g}w^{p}}}\right)(y)}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\tilde{\xi}

(9.35)

(one can just take
\tilde{\xi}=\nu^{-p}\circ\eta_{g}\circ\nu^{p}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}\circ\xi\circ\beta_{g^{-1}}^{U}\circ\nu^{-p}\circ\eta_{g}^{-1}\circ\nu^{p}
). This implies that

\phi_{1}(\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w^{-p}W_{g}w^{p}}}\right)(x))=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\nu^{-p}\circ\eta_{g}\circ\nu^{p}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}(A)\tilde{a}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w^{-p}W_{g}w^{p}}}\right)(y)\tilde{a}^{\dagger}.

(9.36)

Filling this in gives (where we’ve used
\tilde{W}_{g}\vcentcolon=w^{-p}W_{g}w^{p}
and
\tilde{\eta}_{g}\vcentcolon=\nu^{-p}\circ\eta_{g}\circ\nu^{p}
for notational simplicity)

\displaystyle\phi_{1}(\tilde{W}_{g}x\tilde{W}_{g}^{\dagger})\phi_{2}(\tilde{W}_{g})\phi_{1}(x)^{\dagger}\phi_{2}(\tilde{W}_{g})^{\dagger}

(9.37)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}(A)\tilde{a}\tilde{W}_{g}\text{\sout{$y\tilde{W}_{g}^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}$}}\text{\sout{$\tilde{a}\tilde{W}_{g}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}\tilde{a}y^{\dagger}$}}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}(A)^{\dagger}\tilde{a}\tilde{W}_{g}^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}.

To prove that this is the identity operator observe that

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{a}\tilde{W}_{g}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}(A)=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{a}\tilde{W}_{g}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}(A)

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{a}\tilde{W}_{g}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\Phi(A)=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{a}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}\circ\Phi(A)

(9.38)

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{a}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\Phi\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}(A)=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a}}\right)\circ\Phi\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}(A)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}(A).

Filling this in equation (
9.37
) concludes the proof of the first item. The proof of the last item looks very similar. Now take
x\in\textrm{IAC}_{R}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta,\Theta)
arbitrary. Take
A\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{R})
and
y\in\text{Cone}_{R}^{W}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
such that
x=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(A)y
. We now get that (the part with
v
replaced with
v^{\dagger}
and
\tau
by
\tau^{-1}
is equivalent)

vxv^{\dagger}=\pi\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\tau(A)vyv^{\dagger}.

(9.39)

When we now take
\tilde{\xi}=\tau\circ\xi\circ\tau^{-1}
we still obtain an equation of the form

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(vyv^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\tilde{\xi}.

(9.40)

This again (in analogy to equation (
9.36
)) implies that we can take

\phi_{1}(vxv^{\dagger})=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\tau(A)\tilde{a}vyv^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}.

(9.41)

Filling this in now gives

\displaystyle\phi_{1}(vxv^{\dagger})v\phi_{1}(x)^{\dagger}v^{\dagger}

(9.42)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\tau(A)\tilde{a}vyv^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}v\tilde{a}y^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}(A)^{\dagger}v^{\dagger}.

(9.43)

Before we proceed we will first show the equation

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{a}vyv^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}v\tilde{a}y^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\tau.

(9.44)

To prove this, observe that

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{a}vyv^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}v\tilde{a}y^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{a}vyv^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}v}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\xi^{-1}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a}}\right).

(9.45)

Now inserting equation (
9.1
) in this gives

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{a}vyv^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}v}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\text{\sout{$(\Phi)^{-1}$}}\circ\xi^{-1}\circ\text{\sout{$\Phi$}}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{a}vyv^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\tau\circ\xi^{-1}\circ\underline{\tau^{-1}\circ\tau}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}

(9.46)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a}}\right)\circ\tau\circ\xi\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\tau\circ\xi^{-1}\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}\circ\tau.

Using again equation (
9.1
) now yields:

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\text{\sout{$\Phi^{-1}$}}\circ\tau\circ\xi\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\text{\sout{$\Phi$}}\circ\tau\circ\xi^{-1}\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}\circ\tau

(9.47)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\text{\sout{$\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\tau\circ\xi\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\tau\circ\xi^{-1}\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}$}}\circ\tau

concluding the proof of equation (
9.44
). We will now show that the expression in equation (
9.43
) is the identity. First, we will show that the
A
’s in equation (
9.43
) cancel. By equation (
9.44
) we get that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{a}vyv^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}v\tilde{a}y^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}(A^{\dagger})=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\tau(A^{\dagger}).

(9.48)

Filling this in shows that the
A
’s cancel. Now, what is left to show is that
\tilde{a}vyv^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}v\tilde{a}y^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}v^{\dagger}=1
which is equivalent to showing that
\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y}}\right)(v^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}v\tilde{a})=v^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}v\tilde{a}
. To show this, first, we use the definitions of these objects to see that this is equivalent to showing that

\xi(\tau^{-1}(a^{\dagger})a)=\tau^{-1}(a^{\dagger})a.

(9.49)

However, since

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau^{-1}(a^{\dagger})a}}\right)=\tau^{-1}\circ\Phi\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}\circ\tau\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\Phi^{-1}=\tau^{-1}\circ\Phi\circ\tau\circ\Phi^{-1},

(9.50)

we get that
\tau^{-1}(a^{\dagger})a\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{W(C_{\theta})})^{\prime}=\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{W(C_{\theta})^{c}})
, where the last equality is again because of section 3 in
[
7
]
. This concludes the proof of
9.49
. The proof of the second item is analogous concluding the proof of this lemma.
∎

9.2
Bringing
w
inside
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}

In the last section, we constructed our morphisms
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
in such a way that we could take the translation operator
v
outside of the morphism (see lemma
9.6
). To prove the invariance of the
H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})
-valued index, this is sufficient and the rest of this section can be skipped. However, if we want to show the invariance of the
H^{1}(G,\mathbb{T})
-valued index under LGAs generated by a one-parameter family of interactions
H\in\mathcal{B}_{F_{\phi}}([0,1])
that is invariant under both translation symmetries, we need some additional structure. We need to have a mechanism to bring the additional translation operator,
w
outside of the morphisms
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
.

Lemma 9.7
.

There exist maps

\displaystyle A_{L}

\displaystyle:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{L}):\lambda\mapsto A_{L}(\lambda)

\displaystyle A_{R}

\displaystyle:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{R}):\lambda\mapsto A_{R}(\lambda)

(9.51)

both continuous in norm topology and

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mu\nu}:[0,1]\rightarrow\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{W(C_{\theta})^{c}\cap\mu\cap\nu}}}\right):\lambda\mapsto\Phi^{\mu\nu}(\lambda)

(9.52)

where
\mu\in\{U,D\}
and
\nu\in\{L,\nu(R)\}
, all four continuous in strong
19
19

19

Meaning that
\Phi^{\mu\nu}(\lambda)(A)
is continuous for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
.
topology, satisfying that

\displaystyle\nu\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;\lambda}\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{\lambda;0}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{H_{L}}_{0;\lambda}\otimes\gamma^{H_{\nu\circ\nu(R)}}_{0;\lambda}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{\lambda;0}

(9.53)

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{L}(\lambda)}}\right)\otimes\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{R}(\lambda)}}\right)\circ\bigotimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\{U,D\},\\
\nu\in\{L,\nu(R)\}\end{subarray}}\Phi^{\mu\nu}(\lambda)

(9.54)

and that
\Phi^{\mu\nu}(\lambda)\circ\beta_{g}=\beta_{g}\circ\Phi^{\mu\nu}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

This lemma is proven in
B.7
which was based on similar statements made in
[
10
]
.
∎

In what follows, let
A_{L}\vcentcolon=A_{L}(1)
,
A_{R}\vcentcolon=A_{R}(1)
and
\Phi^{\mu\nu}=\Phi^{\mu\nu}(1)
. We will now show some properties for these operators. The first one is that they can indeed be used to exchange
w
and
\phi_{1}/\phi_{2}
:

Lemma 9.8
.

Define
a_{L}\vcentcolon=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(A_{L})
and
a_{R}\vcentcolon=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(A_{R})
, then for any
K\in\text{Cone}_{L\times\nu(R)}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
we get that

w^{\dagger}\phi_{1}(K)w=\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}\>a_{L}\otimes a_{R}\>K\>a_{L}^{\dagger}\otimes a_{R}^{\dagger}\>w).

(9.55)

Similarly, we have that

w^{\dagger}\phi_{2}(W_{g})w=\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}\>a_{L}\otimes a_{R}\>w)\phi_{2}(w^{\dagger}W_{g}w)\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}\>a_{L}^{\dagger}\otimes a_{R}^{\dagger}\>w).

(9.56)

Proof.

We will only show the first equality as the second one is completely analogous. We need to show that

\displaystyle\phi_{1}(K)

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}a_{L}\otimes a_{R}w)}}\right)(\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}Kw))=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}a_{L}\otimes a_{R}w)}}\right)(\tilde{a}w^{\dagger}Kw\tilde{a}^{\dagger})

(9.57)

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}a_{L}\otimes a_{R}w)}}\right)(\tilde{a}w^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}\phi_{1}(K)\tilde{a}^{\dagger}w\tilde{a}^{\dagger})=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}a_{L}\otimes a_{R}w)w^{\dagger}(w\tilde{a}w^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger})}}\right)(\phi_{1}(K)).

To do this, we first find an
A\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
such that

\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}(A)=w\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}a_{L}\otimes a_{R}w)w^{\dagger}(w\tilde{a}w^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger})

(9.58)

and then we will prove that
A\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{W(C_{\theta})^{c}})
. It should be clear that the result follows from this
20
20

20

This is most easily seen from

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\phi_{1}(K)}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}(A)=\phi_{1}(\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(A))=\phi_{1}(\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(A))=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}(A).

(9.59)

. Finding this
A
can be done by working out

w\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}a_{L}\otimes a_{R}w)w^{\dagger}(w\tilde{a}w^{\dagger}\tilde{a}^{\dagger})=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\nu\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\nu^{-1}(A_{L}\otimes A_{R})\nu(a)a^{\dagger}}}\right).

(9.60)

First we insert
\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}=\text{Id}
to obtain

=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\underline{\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\nu\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\nu^{-1}(A_{L}\otimes A_{R})\nu(a)a^{\dagger}}}\right).

(9.61)

Using the fact that
H
is translation invariant allows us to cancel it giving

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}(\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\text{\sout{$\gamma^{H}_{1;0}$}}\circ\nu\circ\text{\sout{$\gamma^{H}_{0;1}$}}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\nu^{-1}(A_{L}\otimes A_{R})\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}(\nu(a)a^{\dagger}))

(9.62)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}(\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\nu\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\nu^{-1}(A_{L}\otimes A_{R})\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}(\nu(a)a^{\dagger})).

This shows that we can take

A\vcentcolon=\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\nu\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\nu^{-1}(A_{L}\otimes A_{R})\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}(\nu(a)a^{\dagger}).

(9.63)

Now we only have to show that indeed
A\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{W(C_{\theta})^{c}})
. To do this, take
X\in\mathcal{A}_{W(C_{\theta})}
arbitrary. If we can show that
\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A}}\right)(X)=X
for any such
X
then we get that
A\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{W(C_{\theta})}^{\prime})
. Since by corollary 3.1 of
[
7
]
we get that
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{W(C_{\theta})}^{\prime})=\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{W(C_{\theta})^{c}})
this is sufficient to conclude the proof. To show this, observe that

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A}}\right)(X)

\displaystyle=\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\nu\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{L}\otimes A_{R}}}\right)\circ\nu\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\text{\sout{$\nu^{-1}$}}\circ\text{\sout{$\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}$}}

(9.64)

\displaystyle\qquad\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}\circ\text{\sout{$\nu$}}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a}}\right)\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}(X)

where the cancellation is done using the fact that
H
is translation invariant. Using equation (
9.54
) we obtain

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A}}\right)(X)=(\bigotimes_{\mu\nu}\Phi^{\mu\nu})^{-1}\circ\nu\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a}}\right)\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}(X).

(9.65)

Now using equation (
9.1
) we get

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A}}\right)(X)=(\bigotimes_{\mu\nu}\Phi^{\mu\nu})^{-1}\circ\nu\circ(\Phi^{U}\otimes\Phi^{D})^{-1}\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\Phi^{U}\otimes\Phi^{D}(X)=X

(9.66)

concluding the proof.
∎

We will now prove a small lemma that we will need to prove lemma
9.10
.

Lemma 9.9
.

For all
\sigma\in\{L,R\}
, the following equality holds:

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)(a_{\sigma}^{\dagger}va_{\sigma}v^{\dagger})=a_{\sigma}^{\dagger}va_{\sigma}v^{\dagger}.

(9.67)

Proof.

We will prove this equality in two steps. First, we will find some
B_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
such that
\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(B_{\sigma})=a_{\sigma}^{\dagger}va_{\sigma}v^{\dagger}
, then we will show that
B_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{A}_{(W(C_{\theta})\cap\sigma)^{c}}
(his already proves invariance under
\eta_{g}
) and that
\beta_{g}^{U}(B_{\sigma})=B_{\sigma}
(concluding the proof of being invariant under
\eta_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}
). To find
B_{\sigma}
, we write out

a_{\sigma}^{\dagger}va_{\sigma}v^{\dagger}=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(A_{\sigma}^{\dagger}\tau(A_{\sigma}))

(9.68)

and observe that we can take
B_{\sigma}\vcentcolon=A_{\sigma}^{\dagger}\tau(A_{\sigma})
. Now we will prove that
B_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{C_{\theta}^{c}\cap\sigma})=\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{(C_{\theta}^{c}\cap\sigma)^{c}}^{\prime})
. Take
X\in\mathcal{A}_{(C_{\theta}^{c}\cap\sigma)^{c}}
arbitrary. Using equation (
9.54
) we have that

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B_{L}\otimes B_{R}}}\right)(X)

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{L}^{\dagger}\otimes A_{R}^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\tau\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{L}\otimes A_{R}}}\right)\circ\tau^{-1}(X)

(9.69)

\displaystyle=\bigotimes_{\mu\nu}\Phi^{\mu\nu}\circ\text{\sout{$\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\nu\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\nu^{-1}$}}

(9.70)

\displaystyle\qquad\circ\tau\circ\text{\sout{$\nu\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}$}}\circ(\bigotimes_{\mu\nu}\Phi^{\mu\nu})^{-1}\circ\tau^{-1}(X)=X

concluding the proof. The fact that
\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta_{g}^{U}(B_{\sigma})}}\right)=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B_{\sigma}}}\right)
follows from this same equation as well. This already shows that that there exists a
G
-dependent phase
\alpha(g)
such that
\beta_{g}^{U}(B_{\sigma})B_{\sigma}^{\dagger}=\alpha(g)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}}
. To show that
\alpha(g)=1
we have to use two things. First, we use the fact that
\alpha
has to be a
U(1)
-representation. Secondly, because we can make this construction for every
\lambda
(I mean the
\lambda
from lemma
9.7
) we need that
\alpha
must be connected to the trivial
U(1)
-representation through a continuous path. However, because our group is finite and finite groups have discrete
U(1)
-representations (see e.g. lemma
4.3
) we have that
\alpha
is trivial, and hence the result follows.
∎

We conclude this subsection by showing that the following lemma holds:

Lemma 9.10
.

Define
\epsilon^{L}_{g}\in\textrm{IAC}_{L}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
and
\epsilon^{R}_{g}\in\textrm{IAC}_{R}(\alpha_{0},\theta)
through

\displaystyle\epsilon_{g}^{\sigma}\vcentcolon=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\nu^{-1}(A_{\sigma}\>\eta_{g}^{\sigma}\circ\beta_{g}^{U\sigma}(A_{\sigma}^{\dagger}))

(9.71)

(where
A_{\sigma}
was defined in lemma
9.7
) then

\displaystyle w^{\dagger}\phi_{2}(W_{g})w

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(\epsilon_{g}^{L}\otimes\epsilon_{g}^{R})\phi_{2}(w^{\dagger}W_{g}w)

\displaystyle w^{\dagger}\phi_{1}(K_{g}^{\sigma})w

\displaystyle=v^{\dagger}\phi_{1}(\epsilon_{g}^{\sigma})v\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}K_{g}^{\sigma}w)\phi_{1}(\epsilon_{g}^{\sigma})^{\dagger}.

(9.72)

Proof.

By equation (
9.56
) we get that

\displaystyle w^{\dagger}\phi_{2}(W_{g})w

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}\>a_{L}\otimes a_{R}\>w)\phi_{2}(w^{\dagger}W_{g}w)\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}\>a_{L}^{\dagger}\otimes a_{R}^{\dagger}\>w)

(9.73)

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}\>a_{L}\otimes a_{R}\>w)\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\phi_{2}(w^{\dagger}W_{g}w)}}\right)(\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}\>a_{L}^{\dagger}\otimes a_{R}^{\dagger}\>w))\phi_{2}(w^{\dagger}W_{g}w).

(9.74)

Using result 2 and 3 of lemma
9.6
on this now gives

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}\>a_{L}\otimes a_{R}\>\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)(a_{L}^{\dagger}\otimes a_{R}^{\dagger})w)\phi_{2}(w^{\dagger}W_{g}w)

(9.75)

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}a_{L}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)(a_{L}^{\dagger})\otimes a_{R}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)(a_{R}^{\dagger})w)\phi_{2}(w^{\dagger}W_{g}w).

(9.76)

Since

\displaystyle a_{\sigma}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)(a_{\sigma}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta(A_{\sigma}\eta_{g}^{\sigma}\circ\beta_{g}^{\sigma U}(A_{\sigma}^{\dagger}))=w\epsilon_{g}^{\sigma}w^{\dagger}

(9.77)

the first result follows. For the second result, we start by using equation (
9.55
) which gives us

\displaystyle w^{\dagger}\phi_{1}(K_{g}^{\sigma})w

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}a_{L}\otimes a_{R}K_{g}^{\sigma}a_{L}^{\dagger}\otimes a_{R}^{\dagger}w)=\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}a_{\sigma}K_{g}^{\sigma}a_{\sigma}^{\dagger}w).

(9.78)

Inserting
v^{\dagger}W_{g}va_{\sigma}^{\dagger}a_{\sigma}v^{\dagger}W_{g}^{\dagger}v=\mathbbm{1}
in this gives us

=\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}a_{\sigma}\underline{\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v^{\dagger}W_{g}v}}\right)(a_{\sigma}^{\dagger}a_{\sigma})}K_{g}^{\sigma}a_{\sigma}^{\dagger}w)=\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}a_{\sigma}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v^{\dagger}W_{g}v}}\right)(a_{\sigma}^{\dagger})K_{g}^{\sigma}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((K_{g}^{\text{\sout{$\sigma$}}})^{\dagger}v^{\dagger}W_{g}v}}\right)(a_{\sigma})a_{\sigma}^{\dagger}w).

(9.79)

We now use the fact that
K_{g}W_{g}=v^{\dagger}W_{g}v
to get

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}a_{\sigma}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v^{\dagger}W_{g}v}}\right)(a_{\sigma}^{\dagger})K_{g}^{\sigma}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)(a_{\sigma})a_{\sigma}^{\dagger}w)

(9.80)

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}a_{\sigma}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v^{\dagger}W_{g}v}}\right)(a_{\sigma}^{\dagger})K_{g}^{\sigma}(\epsilon_{g}^{\sigma})^{\dagger}w).

(9.81)

Inserting
ww^{\dagger}
in this and using lemma
9.6
gives

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}a_{\sigma}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v^{\dagger}W_{g}v}}\right)(a_{\sigma}^{\dagger})\underline{ww^{\dagger}}K_{g}^{\sigma}\underline{ww^{\dagger}}(\epsilon_{g}^{\sigma})^{\dagger}w)

(9.82)

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}a_{\sigma}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v^{\dagger}W_{g}v}}\right)(a_{\sigma}^{\dagger})w)\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}K_{g}^{\sigma}w)\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}(\epsilon_{g}^{\sigma})^{\dagger}w)

(9.83)

\displaystyle=\underline{v^{\dagger}}\phi_{1}(\underline{v}w^{\dagger}a_{\sigma}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v^{\dagger}W_{g}v}}\right)(a_{\sigma}^{\dagger})w\underline{v^{\dagger}})\underline{v}\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}K_{g}^{\sigma}w)\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}(\epsilon_{g}^{\sigma})^{\dagger}w).

(9.84)

The only thing we now have to prove is that

a_{\sigma}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v^{\dagger}W_{g}v}}\right)(a_{\sigma}^{\dagger})=v^{\dagger}a_{\sigma}\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)(a_{\sigma}^{\dagger})v=v^{\dagger}w\epsilon_{g}^{\sigma}w^{\dagger}v

(9.85)

since inserting this, concludes the proof. To do this notice that we can rewrite equation (
9.85
) as

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v^{\dagger}W_{g}v}}\right)(v^{\dagger}a_{\sigma}^{\dagger}va_{\sigma})=v^{\dagger}a_{\sigma}^{\dagger}va_{\sigma}.

(9.86)

This equality is proven in lemma
9.9
.
∎

9.3
Operators belonging to
\omega\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}

The goal of this subsection is to take automorphisms and operators belonging to
\omega
and relate them to operators belonging to
\omega\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}
. To this end, take
\alpha\in\textrm{QAut}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
such that
\omega=\omega_{0}\circ\alpha
, for some product state,
\omega_{0}
. Take
0<\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}<\theta_{3}<\pi/2
arbitrary. Take
\alpha_{L}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{L}}}\right),\alpha_{R}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{R}}}\right),V_{1}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
and
\Theta\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{W(C_{\theta_{3}})^{c}}}}\right)
such that

\alpha=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{1}}}\right)\circ\alpha_{L}\otimes\alpha_{R}\circ\Theta.

(9.87)

Take
\tilde{\beta}_{g,1}
such that
\omega\circ\tilde{\beta}_{g,1}=\omega
and such that there exists some
V_{2,g}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
and some
\eta_{g}^{\sigma}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\nu^{\sigma}(C_{\theta_{1}}\cap\sigma)}}}\right)
satisfying

\tilde{\beta}_{g,1}=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{2,g}}}\right)\circ\eta_{g}^{L}\otimes\eta_{g}^{R}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}.

(9.88)

Take
W_{g},u_{L/R}(g,h),v
and
K_{g}^{L/R}
operators belonging to these automorphisms (see lemmas
5.7
and
5.9
). Clearly
\alpha\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}
satisfies
\omega\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}=\omega_{0}\circ\alpha\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}
. We also have that
\alpha\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\in\textrm{QAut}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
. To show this notice that

\displaystyle\alpha\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{1}}}\right)\circ(\alpha_{L}\circ\gamma^{H_{L}}_{0;1}\otimes\alpha_{R}\circ\gamma^{H_{R}}_{0;1})\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}

(9.89)

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{1}}}\right)\circ(\alpha_{L}\circ\gamma^{H_{L}}_{0;1}\otimes\alpha_{R}\circ\gamma^{H_{R}}_{0;1})\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}.

(9.90)

Because of lemmas
B.4
and
B.3
part 2 (which where based on similar lemmas in
[
10
]
) there exists a
\tilde{\Theta}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{W(C_{\theta_{2}})^{c}}}}\right)
and some
\tilde{V}_{1},A_{1}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
such that

\displaystyle\alpha\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{V}_{1}}}\right)\circ(\alpha_{L}\circ\gamma^{H_{L}}_{0;1}\otimes\alpha_{R}\circ\gamma^{H_{R}}_{0;1})\circ\tilde{\Theta}

(9.91)

\displaystyle(\alpha_{L}\circ\gamma^{H_{L}}_{0;1}\otimes\alpha_{R}\circ\gamma^{H_{R}}_{0;1})\circ\tilde{\Theta}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{1}}}\right)\circ\alpha_{L}\otimes\alpha_{R}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}.

(9.92)

If we now define the automorphism
\tilde{\beta}_{g,2}=\gamma^{H}_{1;0}\circ\tilde{\beta}_{g,1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}
then this indeed satisfies that
\omega\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\tilde{\beta}_{g,2}=\omega\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}
. Define
\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{\sigma}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\nu^{\sigma}(C_{\theta_{2}}\cap\sigma)}}}\right)
through lemma
B.6
(which was again based on similar lemmas from
[
10
]
). It satisfies that there exists a
\tilde{V}_{2,g}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}),A_{2,g}^{\sigma}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)})
such that

\displaystyle\gamma^{H}_{1;0}\tilde{\beta}_{g}\gamma^{H}_{0;1}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{V}_{2,g}}}\right)\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{L}\otimes\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{R}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}

(9.93)

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{2,g}^{\sigma}}}\right)\circ\gamma^{H_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)}}_{1;0}\circ\eta_{g}^{\sigma}\circ\beta_{g}^{\sigma U}\circ\gamma^{H_{\nu^{\sigma}(\sigma)}}_{0;1}\circ(\beta_{g}^{\sigma U})^{-1}.

(9.94)

Take some
a
and
\Phi
like in equation (
9.1
) and use this to define
\phi_{1}
, the group homomorphism defined from lemma
9.3
and
\phi_{2}
, the map defined in lemma
9.5
. Now take
A_{1}
as in equation (
9.92
) and define
\delta_{g}^{\sigma}\vcentcolon=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H_{\sigma}}_{0;1}(A^{\sigma}_{2,g})
where
A^{\sigma}_{2,g}
is as defined in equation (
9.94
). The following lemma now holds:

Lemma 9.11
.

The following operators belong to
\omega\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}:

\displaystyle\tilde{u}_{\sigma}(g,h)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}(A_{1})\phi_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\delta^{\sigma}_{g}W_{g}\delta^{\sigma}_{h}W_{g}^{\dagger}u_{\sigma}(g,h)(\delta^{\sigma}_{gh})^{\dagger}}}\right)\pi_{0}(A_{1})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\tilde{v}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}(A_{1})v\pi_{0}(A_{1})^{\dagger}

(9.95)

\displaystyle\tilde{W}_{g}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}(A_{1})\phi_{1}(\delta^{L}_{g}\otimes\delta^{R}_{g})\phi_{2}(W_{g})\pi_{0}(A_{1}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\tilde{K}_{g}^{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}(A_{1})\phi_{1}(v^{\dagger}\delta_{g}^{\sigma}vK_{g}^{\sigma}(\delta_{g}^{\sigma})^{\dagger})\pi_{0}(A_{1})^{\dagger}.

(9.96)

Proof.

To do this, we first state that a small calculation shows that
\forall x\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
, satisfying that
\exists\xi\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
, such that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\xi,

(9.97)

we get that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi_{0}(A_{1})x\pi_{0}(A_{1}^{\dagger})}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\tilde{\Theta}=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\tilde{\Theta}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}\circ\xi\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}.

(9.98)

This already proves that

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi_{0}(A_{1})v\pi_{0}(A_{1}^{\dagger})}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\tilde{\Theta}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\tilde{\Theta}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}\circ\tau\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}

(9.99)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\tilde{\Theta}\circ\tau.

(9.100)

To show the second one we get that

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\phi_{1}(\delta_{g})\phi_{2}(W_{g})}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta

(9.101)

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\phi_{1}(\delta_{g})}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\eta_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}

(9.102)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\gamma^{H_{L}}_{0;1}(A_{2,g}^{L})\otimes\gamma^{H_{R}}_{0;1}(A_{2,g}^{R})}}\right)\circ\eta_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}

(9.103)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{2,g}^{L}\otimes A_{2,g}^{R}}}\right)\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\eta_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}.

(9.104)

Using equation (
9.94
) on this we obtain

=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}.

(9.105)

Inserting this in equation (
9.98
) with
x=\phi_{1}(\delta_{g})\phi_{2}(W_{g})
and
\xi=\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}
now proves the fact that
\tilde{W}_{g}
is an operator belonging to
\omega\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}
. Proving that
\tilde{u}_{\sigma}(g,h)
and
\tilde{K}_{g}^{\sigma}
are operators belonging to
\omega\circ\gamma_{0;1}^{H}
is completely analogous. This concludes the proof.
∎

Additionally, in the case where both
\omega
and
H
are translation invariant in both directions, take
w
,
b_{g}^{L/R}
, and
h_{g}
, the additional operators belonging to this state. Take
\epsilon_{g}^{\sigma}
as defined in lemma
9.10
. The following lemma now holds:

Lemma 9.12
.

If both
\omega
and
H
are translation invariant in both directions then, additionally to the operators presented in lemma
9.11
, the following operators belong to
\omega\circ\gamma_{0;1}^{H}
as well:

\tilde{w}=\pi_{0}(A_{1})w\pi_{0}(A_{1})^{\dagger}\quad\tilde{b}_{g}^{\sigma}=\pi_{0}(A_{1})w^{\dagger}\phi_{1}(\delta_{g}^{R})w\phi_{1}(\epsilon_{g}^{R})\phi_{1}(b_{g}^{R})\phi_{1}(\delta_{g}^{R})^{\dagger}\pi_{0}(A_{1})^{\dagger}\quad\tilde{h}_{g}=\pi_{0}(A_{1})\phi_{1}(h_{g})\pi_{0}(A_{1}).

(9.106)

Proof.

The proof that the first four operators belong to
\omega\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}
is completely analogous to what was done in the proof of theorem
9.11
. We will therefore focus on the
\tilde{b}_{g}^{\sigma}
part (the other two are trivial). To show this, observe that we have

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{\phi}_{1}(\epsilon_{g}^{R}b_{g}^{R})}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta

(9.107)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{R}\>\eta_{g}^{R}\circ\beta_{g}^{RU}(A_{R}^{\dagger})}}\right)\circ\text{\sout{$\nu\circ\nu^{-1}$}}\circ\eta_{g}^{R}\circ\nu\circ(\eta_{g}^{R})^{-1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}

(9.108)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{R}\>\eta_{g}^{R}\circ\beta_{g}^{RU}(A_{R}^{\dagger})}}\right)\circ\eta_{g}^{R}\circ\underline{\beta_{g}^{RU}}\circ\nu

(9.109)

\displaystyle\qquad\circ\underline{(\beta_{g}^{\nu^{-1}(RU)})^{-1}}\circ(\eta_{g}^{R})^{-1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{R}}}\right)\circ\eta_{g}^{R}\circ\beta_{g}^{RU}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{R}^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\nu

(9.110)

\displaystyle\qquad\circ(\beta_{g}^{\nu^{-1}(RU)})^{-1}\circ(\eta_{g}^{R})^{-1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\underline{\nu^{-1}\circ\nu}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{R}}}\right)\circ\eta_{g}^{R}\circ\beta_{g}^{RU}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{R}^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\nu\circ\underline{\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}}

(9.111)

\displaystyle\qquad\circ\underline{\nu^{-1}\circ\nu\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}}\circ(\beta_{g}^{\nu^{-1}(RU)})^{-1}\circ(\eta_{g}^{R})^{-1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}.

After using the restriction to
R
of equation (
9.54
), this leads to

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\underline{\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\text{\sout{$(\otimes_{\mu}\Phi^{\mu\nu(R)})^{-1}$}}}\circ\eta_{g}^{R}\circ\beta_{g}^{RU}\circ\underline{\text{\sout{$(\otimes_{\mu}\Phi^{\mu\nu(R)})$}}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}}\circ\nu

(9.112)

\displaystyle\qquad\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ(\beta_{g}^{\nu^{-1}(RU)})^{-1}\circ(\eta_{g}^{R})^{-1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}.

On the other hand, we have that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w^{\dagger}\tilde{\phi}_{1}(\delta_{g}^{\sigma})w}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A^{\sigma}_{2,g}}}\right)\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}\circ\nu.

(9.113)

This leads us to

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w^{\dagger}\tilde{\phi}_{1}(\delta_{g}^{R})w\tilde{\phi}_{1}(\epsilon_{g}^{R}b_{g}^{R})}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta

(9.114)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{2,g}^{R}}}\right)\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\eta_{g}^{R}\circ\beta_{g}^{RU}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\nu

(9.115)

\displaystyle\qquad\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ(\beta_{g}^{\nu^{-1}(RU)})^{-1}\circ(\eta_{g}^{R})^{-1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{R}\circ\beta_{g}^{RU}\circ\nu\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ(\beta_{g}^{\nu^{-1}(RU)})^{-1}\circ(\eta_{g}^{R})^{-1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}.

(9.116)

Now define
S=\{(0,y)|y\in\mathbb{Z}\}
then

=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{R}\circ\beta_{g}^{\nu(RU)}\circ\text{\sout{$\beta_{g}^{S}$}}\circ\nu\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ\text{\sout{$(\beta_{g}^{\nu^{-1}(S)})^{-1}$}}\circ(\beta_{g}^{RU})^{-1}\circ(\eta_{g}^{R})^{-1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}.

(9.117)

Doing the same thing on the right will give us

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w^{\dagger}\tilde{\phi}_{1}(\delta_{g}^{R})w\tilde{\phi}_{1}(\epsilon_{g}^{R}b_{g}^{R})\tilde{\phi}_{1}(\delta_{g}^{R})^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta

(9.118)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{R}\circ\beta_{g}^{\nu(RU)}\circ\nu\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{1;0}\circ(\beta_{g}^{RU})^{-1}\circ(\eta_{g}^{R})^{-1}\circ\gamma^{{H_{\text{split}}}}_{0;1}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((A_{2,g}^{R})^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}

(9.119)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{R}\circ\text{\sout{$\beta_{g}^{\nu(RU)}$}}\circ\nu\circ\text{\sout{$(\beta_{g}^{RU})^{-1}$}}\circ(\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{R})^{-1}\circ\circ\gamma^{H}_{1;0}.

(9.120)

The role that the
A_{1}
will play is analogous to the proof of theorem
9.13
(see equation (
9.98
)), concluding the proof.
∎

9.4
The indices are invariant under LGAs

Theorem 9.13
.

\textrm{Index}(\omega)=\textrm{Index}(\omega\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1})

Proof.

To show this, we merely have to show that the index is invariant under the transformations indicated in lemma
9.11
. The fact that the index is invariant under
\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi_{0}(A_{1})}}\right)
and under
\phi(\cdot)
is just because both transformations are homomorphisms. To show that it is invariant under the transformation with de
\delta^{\sigma}_{g}
we invoke lemma
7.3
.
∎

Theorem 9.14
.

\textrm{Index}_{\text{2 trans}}(\omega)=\textrm{Index}_{\text{2 trans}}(\omega\circ\gamma^{H}_{0;1}).

Proof.

To show this, we merely have to show that the index is invariant under the transformations indicated in lemma
9.11
and
9.12
. Let
\alpha\in\hom(G,U(1))
be the old index and
\tilde{\alpha}\in\hom(G,U(1))
the new one. We have that

\displaystyle\alpha(g)

\displaystyle=v^{\dagger}b_{g}^{R}vh_{g}K_{g}^{R}(b_{g}^{R})^{\dagger}w^{\dagger}(K_{g}^{R})^{\dagger}w

(9.121)

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(v^{\dagger}b_{g}^{R}vh_{g}K_{g}^{R}(b_{g}^{R})^{\dagger}w^{\dagger}(K_{g}^{R})^{\dagger}w)

(9.122)

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(v^{\dagger}b_{g}^{R}vh_{g}K_{g}^{R}(b_{g}^{R})^{\dagger})\phi_{1}(w^{\dagger}(K_{g}^{R})^{\dagger}w).

(9.123)

Using equation (
9.72
) on this gives us

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(v^{\dagger}b_{g}^{R}vh_{g}K_{g}^{R}(b_{g}^{R})^{\dagger})\phi_{1}((\epsilon_{g}^{R})^{\dagger})w^{\dagger}\phi_{1}((K_{g}^{R})^{\dagger})wv^{\dagger}\phi_{1}(\epsilon_{g}^{R})v

(9.124)

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(v^{\dagger}\epsilon_{g}^{R}b_{g}^{R}vh_{g}K_{g}^{R}(b_{g}^{R})^{\dagger}(\epsilon_{g}^{R})^{\dagger})w^{\dagger}\phi_{1}((K_{g}^{R})^{\dagger})w.

(9.125)

This shows that the index is invariant under the
\phi_{1}
and
\epsilon^{R}_{g}
part (simultaneously). The fact that the index is invariant under the
\phi_{1}(\delta_{g})
part is now completely equivalent to the proof of lemma
7.4
. The fact that the index remains unchanged by the
\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi_{0}(A_{1})}}\right)
transformation is trivial. This concludes the proof.
∎

10
The
H^{1}(G,\mathbb{T})
-valued index is invariant under rotations by
90^{\circ}

Take

\omega\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\omega\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\begin{matrix}\omega\text{ is an SRE state,}\\
\omega\circ\beta_{g}=\omega,\>\omega\circ\tau=\omega\text{ and }\omega\circ\nu=\omega\end{matrix}}}\right.}}\right\}

(10.1)

arbitrary and let
\mu
be the automorphism that rotates the lattice by
90^{\circ}
. This section will be dedicated to proving that

\textrm{Index}_{\text{2 trans}}^{\mathcal{A},U(g)}(\omega)=\textrm{Index}_{\text{2 trans}}^{\mathcal{A},U(g)}(\omega\circ\mu).

(10.2)

For the
H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})
-valued index, the similar statement is not true, that is why in equation (
1.6
), we have two
H^{2}(G,\mathbb{T})
-valued indices. This is because if we have two translation symmetries,
\omega\mapsto\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U(g)}(\omega)
and
\omega\mapsto\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U(g)}(\omega\circ\mu)
are both well-defined indices.

In what follows, let
\omega_{0}
be the product state and let
\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}
be the disentangler such that
\omega=\omega_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}
. Sometimes we will need a specific decomposition of this locally generated Automorphism:

1
2
3
4

Figure 4
:
This figure indicates four different areas of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and we will take
\pi_{0}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{4}\pi_{0,i}
where each of the
\pi_{0,i}
is an irreducible representation of the restriction of
\mathcal{A}
to each of the areas. The red areas are chosen such that they do not overlap with the support of
\tilde{\eta}_{g}
and
\eta_{g}
.

Lemma 10.1
.

Let
\{1,2,3,4\}
be regions indicated in figure
4
(including the red areas) and let
\mathcal{A}_{\text{red}}
be the part of the
C^{*}
algebra that has support in the red regions of figure
4
. There exists a
B\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
and a
\Theta\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\text{red}}}}\right)
such that
\beta^{\mu\nu}_{g}\circ\Theta=\Theta\circ\beta^{\mu\nu}_{g}
(for all
\mu\in\{U,D\}
and
\nu\in\{L,R\}
) satisfying

\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B}}\right)\circ\gamma^{\sum_{i=1}^{4}\Phi_{i}}_{0;1}\circ\Theta

(10.3)

where
\Phi_{i}
is the restriction of
\Phi
to region
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
.

Proof.

Completely analogous to the proof of (for example) lemma
B.3
which was in turn based on similar lemmas in
[
10
]
.
∎

Let
A_{g,1},A_{g,2}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}),\eta^{\sigma}_{g}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\nu^{\sigma}(C_{\theta}\cap\sigma)}}}\right)
and
\eta^{\rho}_{2,g}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\tau^{\rho}(\mu(C_{\theta})\cap\rho)}}}\right)
be such that the automorphisms

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}_{g,1}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{g,1}}}\right)\circ\eta_{g}^{L}\otimes\eta_{g}^{R}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}_{g,2}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{g,2}}}\right)\circ\eta_{2,g}^{U}\otimes\eta_{2,g}^{D}\circ\beta_{g}^{L}

(10.4)

satisfy
\omega\circ\tilde{\beta}_{g,1}=\omega\circ\tilde{\beta}_{g,2}=\omega
. Let
(\mathcal{H}_{0}=\bigotimes_{i}\mathcal{H}_{0,i},\pi_{0}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{4}\pi_{0,i},\bigotimes_{i}\Omega_{0,i})
be a GNS triple for
\omega_{0}
(see the caption of figure
4
). The following now holds:

Lemma 10.2
.

There exists an
X_{g}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
satisfying that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{g}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}=\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}\circ\tilde{\eta}^{U}_{g}\circ\eta^{R}_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{RU}

(10.5)

where
\tilde{\eta}_{g}=\beta_{g}^{U}\circ\eta_{2,g^{-1}}\circ(\beta_{g}^{U})^{-1}
.

Proof.

Let
W_{g}
and
\tilde{W}_{g}
be such that

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}\circ\eta_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{W}_{g}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}\circ\eta_{2,g}\circ\beta_{g}^{L}

(10.6)

then

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}\tilde{W}_{g^{-1}}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}\circ\eta_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}\circ\eta_{2,g^{-1}}\circ(\beta_{g}^{L})^{-1}

(10.7)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}\circ\eta_{g}\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{RU}\circ(\beta_{g}^{LD})^{-1}.

(10.8)

Now we use lemma
10.1
and obtain

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g}\tilde{W}_{g^{-1}}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B}}\right)\circ\gamma^{\sum_{i}\Phi_{i}}_{0;1}\circ\text{\sout{$\Theta$}}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B}}\right)\circ\gamma^{\sum_{i}\Phi_{i}}_{0;1}\circ\text{\sout{$\Theta$}}\circ\eta_{g}\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{RU}\circ(\beta_{g}^{LD})^{-1}

(10.9)

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi_{0}(B^{\dagger})W_{g}\tilde{W}_{g^{-1}}\pi_{0}(B)}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\sum_{i}\Phi_{i}}_{0;1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\sum_{i}\Phi_{i}}_{0;1}\circ\eta_{g}\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}\circ\beta_{g}^{RU}\circ(\beta_{g}^{LD})^{-1}

(10.10)

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi_{0}(B^{\dagger})W_{g}\tilde{W}_{g^{-1}}\pi_{0}(B)}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi_{0,1}\otimes\pi_{0,2}\circ\gamma^{\Phi_{1}+\Phi_{2}}_{0;1}\circ\eta_{g}^{R}\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{U}\circ\beta_{g}^{RU}\circ\gamma^{\Phi_{1}+\Phi_{2}}_{1;0}}}\right)

(10.11)

\displaystyle\qquad\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi_{0,3}\otimes\pi_{0,4}\circ\gamma^{\Phi_{3}+\Phi_{4}}_{0;1}\circ\eta_{g}^{L}\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{D}\circ(\beta_{g}^{LD})^{-1}\circ\gamma^{\Phi_{3}+\Phi_{4}}_{1;0}}}\right).

By lemma
5.5
, there exist
\tilde{X}_{g}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{0,1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{0,2})
and
\tilde{Y}_{g}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{0,3}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{0,4})
such that
\tilde{X}_{g}\otimes\tilde{Y}_{g}=\pi_{0}(B^{\dagger})W_{g}\tilde{W}_{g^{-1}}\pi_{0}(B)
. Define
X_{g}=\pi_{0}(B)(\tilde{X}_{g}\otimes\mathbbm{1})\pi_{0}(B^{\dagger})
. This indeed satisfies equation (
10.5
) concluding the proof.
∎

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
v,w,K_{g}^{R}
and
b_{g}^{R}
be such that

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)\circ\pi

\displaystyle=\pi\circ\tau

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(K_{g}^{R}}}\right)\circ\pi

\displaystyle=\pi\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\eta_{g}^{R}\circ\beta_{g}^{RU}\circ\tau\circ(\beta_{g}^{RU})^{-1}\circ(\eta_{g}^{R})^{-1}

(10.12)

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)\circ\pi

\displaystyle=\pi\circ\nu

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{g}^{R}}}\right)\circ\pi

\displaystyle=\pi\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\eta_{g}^{R}\circ\nu\circ(\eta_{g}^{R})^{-1}

(10.13)

(where
\pi=\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}
) then

v^{\dagger}b_{g}^{R}vh_{g}K_{g}^{R}(b_{g}^{R})^{\dagger}=\alpha(g)w^{\dagger}K_{g}^{R}w

(10.14)

holds. Moreover
\alpha=\textrm{Index}_{\text{2 trans}}(\omega)
. If one now defines
\tilde{b}_{g}^{R}
and
\tilde{K}_{g}^{U}
such that

\displaystyle\tilde{b}_{g}^{U}K_{g}^{R}X_{g}

\displaystyle=v^{\dagger}X_{g}v

\displaystyle(10.15)

\displaystyle b_{g}^{R}\tilde{K}_{g}^{U}X_{g}

\displaystyle=w^{\dagger}X_{g}w

\displaystyle(10.16)

then

w^{\dagger}\tilde{b}_{g}^{U}wh_{g}\tilde{K}_{g}^{U}(\tilde{b}_{g}^{U})^{\dagger}=\tilde{\alpha}(g)v^{\dagger}\tilde{K}_{g}^{U}v

(10.17)

holds. Moreover
\tilde{\alpha}=\textrm{Index}_{\text{2 trans}}(\omega\circ\mu)
.

Proof.

The existence of the
v,w,K_{g}^{R}
and
b_{g}^{R}
is due to the fact that
\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}\in\textrm{QAut}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
. This follows from lemma
B.3
(which was based on similar lemmas in
[
10
]
) and from the fact that
\textrm{SQAut}_{1}(\mathcal{A})\subset\textrm{QAut}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
(see definition
14.1
). By construction this also implies that indeed
\alpha=\textrm{Index}_{\text{2 trans}}(\omega)
. We now only have to show the second part of the proof. We have that

\displaystyle\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{K}_{g}^{U}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((b_{g}^{R})^{\dagger}w^{\dagger}X_{g}wX_{g}^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}

(10.18)

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((b_{g}^{R})^{\dagger}w^{\dagger}X_{g}w}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}\circ(\beta_{g}^{RU})^{-1}\circ(\eta_{g}^{R})^{-1}\circ(\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{U})^{-1}

(10.19)

\displaystyle=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((b_{g}^{R})^{\dagger}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{U}\circ\eta_{g}^{R}\circ\beta_{g}^{RU}\circ\nu\circ(\beta_{g}^{RU})^{-1}\circ(\eta_{g}^{R})^{-1}\circ(\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{U})^{-1}

(10.20)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{U}\circ\beta_{g}^{RU}\circ\nu\circ(\beta_{g}^{RU})^{-1}\circ(\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{U})^{-1}.

(10.21)

By similar arguments, we get that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{b}_{g}^{U}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}=\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{U}\circ\tau\circ(\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{U})^{-1}.

(10.22)

This also shows that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{K}_{g}^{U}}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}\circ\mu=\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}\circ\mu\circ\mu^{-1}\circ\nu^{-1}\circ\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{U}\circ\beta_{g}^{RU}\circ\nu\circ(\beta_{g}^{RU})^{-1}\circ(\tilde{\eta}_{g}^{U})^{-1}\circ\mu

(10.23)

and similarly for
\tilde{b}_{g}^{U}
and therefore these operators also belong to
\omega\circ\mu
. This together with the independence of the choice of
\tilde{\eta}_{g}
proves the second result.
∎

Lemma 10.4
.

The following equalities hold

\displaystyle[h_{g},w^{\dagger}\tilde{b}_{g}^{U}w]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle(10.24)

\displaystyle[w^{\dagger}\tilde{b}_{g}^{U}w,v^{\dagger}b_{g}^{R}v]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle(10.25)

\displaystyle[h_{g},v^{\dagger}b_{g}^{R}v]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle(10.26)

\displaystyle[K_{g}^{R},\tilde{b}_{g}^{U}]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle(10.27)

\displaystyle[h_{g},K_{g}^{R}]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle(10.28)

\displaystyle[K_{g}^{R},v^{\dagger}\tilde{K}^{U}_{g}v]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle(10.29)

Proof.

All of the above commute as automorphisms on the
C^{*}
algebra, we now have to show that the operators in the GNS space commute as well. First we will start by proving equations (
10.4
), (
10.4
) and (
10.4
). For any operator
x\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
satisfying that there exists a
\xi\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
such that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}=\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}\circ\xi

(10.30)

we get that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)(h_{g})=\pi_{0}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}\circ\xi(U_{(-1,-1)}(g)).

(10.31)

Applying this to these equations gives the desired result. To show the remaining equations decompose
\pi_{0}
and
\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}
as
\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B}}\right)\circ\bigotimes_{i=1}^{4}\gamma^{\Phi_{i}}_{0;1}\circ\Theta
and
\pi_{0}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{4}\pi_{0,i}
where the
\pi_{0,i}
are irreducible representations in
\mathcal{A}_{i}
, the areas
i
are indicated in figure
4
and the
\Theta
have support in the areas shaded red. This shows that up to some inner automorphisms, the operators with an
R
on top act on different parts of the decomposition of
\pi_{0}
than the operators with a
U
on top do. Combining this with lemma
5.5
concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 10.5
.

\tilde{\alpha}(g)=\alpha(g)
.

Proof.

We will work out the expression

h_{g}v^{\dagger}b_{g}^{R}vw^{\dagger}\tilde{b}_{g}^{U}wK_{g}^{R}\tilde{K}_{g}^{U}X_{g}

(10.32)

in two different ways. On the one hand we will use (
10.4
) together with equation (
10.4
) to get

\eqref{eq:H^1IndexRotationInvariantExpression}=\underline{w^{\dagger}\tilde{b}_{g}^{U}wh_{g}v^{\dagger}b_{g}^{R}v}K_{g}^{R}\tilde{K}_{g}^{U}X_{g}.

(10.33)

Now using equation (
10.14
) we get

(
10.32
)

\displaystyle=\alpha(g)w^{\dagger}\tilde{b}_{g}^{U}ww^{\dagger}K_{g}^{R}wb_{g}^{R}\tilde{K}_{g}^{U}X_{g}=\alpha(g)w^{\dagger}\tilde{b}_{g}^{U}K_{g}^{R}X_{g}w=\alpha(g)w^{\dagger}v^{\dagger}X_{g}vw

(10.34)

where we’ve used equation (
10.3
) in the first equality and (
10.3
) in the second equality. On the other hand, starting with equation (
10.4
), (
10.4
) and (
10.4
) gives

(
10.32
)

\displaystyle=\underline{v^{\dagger}b_{g}^{R}vh_{g}}w^{\dagger}\tilde{b}_{g}^{U}wK_{g}^{R}\tilde{K}_{g}^{U}X_{g}=v^{\dagger}b_{g}^{R}v\underline{K_{g}^{R}h_{g}w^{\dagger}\tilde{b}_{g}^{U}w}\tilde{K}_{g}^{U}X_{g}.

(10.35)

Now using equation (
10.17
) we get

(
10.32
)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\alpha}(g)v^{\dagger}b_{g}^{R}vK_{g}^{R}v^{\dagger}\tilde{K}_{g}^{U}v\tilde{b}_{g}^{U}X_{g}=\tilde{\alpha}(g)v^{\dagger}b_{g}^{R}v\underline{v^{\dagger}\tilde{K}_{g}^{U}v\tilde{b}_{g}^{U}K_{g}^{R}}X_{g}

(10.36)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\alpha}(g)v^{\dagger}b_{g}^{R}\text{\sout{$vv^{\dagger}$}}\tilde{K}_{g}^{U}\text{\sout{$vv^{\dagger}$}}X_{g}v=\tilde{\alpha}(g)v^{\dagger}w^{\dagger}X_{g}wv

(10.37)

where we’ve used equation (
10.4
) and (
10.4
) in the first equality, equation (
10.3
) in the second equality and equation (
10.3
) in the last equality. These two results can only be consistent if
\alpha(g)=\tilde{\alpha}(g)
for all
g\in G
.
∎

11
The role of stacking

Take
\mathcal{A}
a quasi-local
C^{*}
algebra on a two-dimensional lattice and take
U\in\hom(G,U(\mathbb{C}^{d}))
an on-site group action arbitrary. The goal of this section is to prove the following theorem:

Theorem 11.1
.

Let
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
satisfy assumption
3.2
then

\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A}^{2},U\otimes U}(\omega_{1}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\omega_{2})=\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\omega_{1})+\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\omega_{2}).

(11.1)

If additionally,
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
satisfy assumption
3.5
then

\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A}^{2},U\otimes U}_{\text{2 trans}}(\omega_{1}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\omega_{2})=\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\omega_{1})+\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}_{\text{2 trans}}(\omega_{2}).

(11.2)

To prove this theorem, let
\omega_{0i}
and
\gamma_{0;1}^{\Phi_{i}}
(for all
i\in\{1,2\}
) be product states and disentanglers satisfying that
\omega_{i}=\omega_{0i}\circ\gamma_{0;1}^{\Phi_{i}}
. Let
(\mathcal{H}_{0i},\pi_{0i},\Omega_{0i})
be GNS triples for the
\omega_{0i}
. Clearly,
(\mathcal{H}_{01}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{02},\pi_{01}\otimes\pi_{02},\Omega_{01}\otimes\Omega_{02})
is a GNS triple for
\omega_{01}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\omega_{02}
. The proof of the theorem therefore follows by using the following lemma:

Lemma 11.2
.

Let
W_{g,i},u^{\sigma}_{i}(g,h),K_{g,i}^{\sigma}
and
v_{i}
be operators belonging to
\omega_{i}
then
W_{g,1}\otimes W_{g,2},u^{\sigma}_{1}(g,h)\otimes u^{\sigma}_{2}(g,h),K_{g,1}^{\sigma}\otimes K_{g,2}^{\sigma}
and
v_{1}\otimes v_{2}
are operators belonging to
\omega_{1}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\omega_{2}
. When
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
are translation invariant in both directions, the similar statement holds for
w,b_{g}^{\sigma}
and
h_{g}
.

Proof.

To show this we simply have to apply the adjoint action of these operators on
\pi_{01}\otimes\pi_{02}
and see that these are indeed implementations of the right automorphisms. This is so by construction.
∎

To conclude the proof theorem
11.1
, observe that

\displaystyle C(g,h)

(11.3)

\displaystyle=(K_{g,1}^{R}\otimes K_{g,2}^{R})\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{g,1}\otimes W_{g,2}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((K_{h,1}^{R}\otimes K_{h,2}^{R})\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((W_{h,1}\otimes W_{h,2})(W_{gh,1}\otimes W_{gh,2})^{\dagger}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((K_{gh,1}^{R}\otimes K_{gh,2}^{R})^{\dagger}}}\right)}}\right)

\displaystyle(u^{R}_{1}(g,h)\otimes u^{R}_{2}(g,h))v^{\dagger}(u^{R}_{1}(g,h)\otimes u^{R}_{2}(g,h))^{\dagger}v=C_{1}(g,h)C_{2}(g,h)

(11.4)

concluding the proof of the first part. The second part is analogous.

Remark 11.3
.

The same argument can be used to argue that the
H^{3}
-valued index is invariant under stacking.

12
The indices are independent on the choice of on-site Hilbert space

Let
V\in U(\mathbb{C}^{d})
be a unitary operator on the on-site Hilbert space and take
i_{V}
as defined in subsection
2.1
. In this section we will prove the following lemma:

Lemma 12.1
.

Let
\omega
satisfy assumption
3.2
then for any choice of automorphisms
(\theta,\tilde{\beta}_{g},\eta_{g},\alpha_{0},\Theta,\omega,\omega_{0})
we get that

\displaystyle\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V^{\dagger}}}\right)(U)}(\theta,i_{V}^{-1}\circ\tilde{\beta}_{g}\circ i_{V},i_{V}^{-1}\circ\eta_{g}\circ i_{V},i_{V}^{-1}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ i_{V},i_{V}^{-1}\circ\Theta\circ i_{V},\omega\circ i_{V},\omega_{0}\circ i_{V})

(12.1)

\displaystyle=\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\theta,\tilde{\beta}_{g},\eta_{g},\alpha_{0},\Theta,\omega,\omega_{0}).

(12.2)

Additionally, when
\omega
satisfies assumption
3.5
, we get that

\displaystyle\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V^{\dagger}}}\right)(U)}_{\textrm{2 trans}}(\theta,i_{V}^{-1}\circ\tilde{\beta}_{g}\circ i_{V},i_{V}^{-1}\circ\eta_{g}\circ i_{V},i_{V}^{-1}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ i_{V},i_{V}^{-1}\circ\Theta\circ i_{V},\omega\circ i_{V},\omega_{0}\circ i_{V})

(12.3)

\displaystyle=\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}_{\textrm{2 trans}}(\theta,\tilde{\beta}_{g},\eta_{g},\alpha_{0},\Theta,\omega,\omega_{0}).

(12.4)

We do this by proving the following lemma:

Lemma 12.2
.

Let
W_{g}
,
u_{R}(g,h)
,
v
and
K^{R}_{g}
be operators belonging to

\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\theta,\tilde{\beta}_{g},\eta_{g},\alpha_{0},\Theta,\omega,\omega_{0})

(12.5)

for a GNS triple for
\omega_{0}
,
(\mathcal{H}_{0},\pi_{0},\Omega_{0})
, then
W_{g}
,
u_{R}(g,h)
,
v
and
K^{R}_{g}
are also operators belonging to

\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A},\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V^{\dagger}}}\right)(U)}(\theta,i_{V}^{-1}\circ\tilde{\beta}_{g}\circ i_{V},i_{V}^{-1}\circ\eta_{g}\circ i_{V},i_{V}^{-1}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ i_{V},i_{V}^{-1}\circ\Theta\circ i_{V},\omega\circ i_{V},\omega_{0}\circ i_{V})

(12.6)

for the GNS triple for
\omega_{0}\circ i_{V}
given by
(\mathcal{H}_{0},\pi_{0}\circ i_{V},\Omega_{0})
. In the case where
\omega
is translation invariant in both directions, the same holds for the additional operators
w
,
b_{g}^{R}
, and
h_{g}
.

Proof.

To show this, we merely have to observe that for any
x\in U(\mathcal{H}_{0})
satisfying that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta=\pi_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ\Theta\circ\xi

(12.7)

for some
\xi\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
we get that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)\circ\pi_{0}\circ i_{V}\circ i_{V}^{-1}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ i_{V}\circ i_{V}^{-1}\circ\Theta\circ i_{V}=\pi_{0}\circ i_{V}\circ i_{V}^{-1}\circ\alpha_{0}\circ i_{V}\circ i_{V}^{-1}\circ\Theta\circ i_{V}\circ i_{V}^{-1}\circ\xi\circ i_{V}

(12.8)

concluding the proof.
∎

The proof that lemma
12.2
implies lemma
12.1
is trivial.

Remark 12.3
.

The same argument can be used to argue that the
H^{3}
-valued index is invariant under the same transformation.

13
Proof of the main theorems

We first summarise the proof of theorem
3.4
:

Proof.

The 2-cochain was defined in subsection
6.1
. The proof that it wasn’t dependent on any of the choices made in assumption
3.2
was done in section
7
. The first item in the list concerning the invariance under locally generated automorphisms was shown in subsection
9.4
. The proof of the second item concerning the relation between the 1d SPT index and the 2d translation SPT index is done in subsection
8.1
. The proof of the third item concerning the stacking is done in section
11
. The proof of the last item concerning a change of basis of the on-site Hilbert space is done in section
12
. This concludes the proof.
∎

We now summarise the proof of theorem
3.7
:

Proof.

The 2-cochain was defined in subsection
6.2
. The proof that it wasn’t dependent on any of the choices made in assumption
3.5
was done in subsection
7
. The first item in the list concerning the invariance under locally generated automorphisms was shown in subsection
9.4
. The proof of the second item concerning the relation between the 1d SPT index and the 2d translation SPT index is done in subsection
8.2
. The proof of the third item concerning the stacking is done in section
11
. The proof of the last item concerning a change of basis of the on-site Hilbert space is done in section
12
. This concludes the proof.
∎

14
Proof of theorem
3.8

Figure 5
:
This figure shows the support of the different automorphisms present in the decomposition of an SQ
1
-automorphism acting on the upper half-plane.

We will use the notation

\displaystyle C_{[0,\theta]}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=C_{\theta}

\displaystyle C_{]\theta_{1},\theta_{2}]}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=C_{\theta_{2}}/C_{\theta_{1}}

\displaystyle C_{]\theta,\pi/2]}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathbb{Z}^{2}/C_{\theta}

(14.1)

where
0<\theta<\pi/2
and
0<\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}<\pi_{2}
. In analogy with section 2.1 from
[
10
]
we will define some classes of automorphisms (see figure
5
):

Definition 14.1
.

Take
\alpha\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
. We say that
\alpha\in\textrm{SQAut}_{1}(\mathcal{A})
if and only if for any
0<\theta_{0.8}<\theta_{1}<\theta_{1.2}<\theta_{1.8}<\theta_{2}<\theta_{2.2}<\theta_{2.8}<\theta_{3}<\theta_{3.2}<\pi/2
there exist
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
\alpha_{[0,\theta_{1}],\sigma}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\nu^{\sigma}(C_{[0,\theta_{1}]}\cap\sigma)}}}\right),\>\alpha_{]\theta_{1},\theta_{2}],\sigma,\rho}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\nu^{\sigma}(C_{]\theta_{1},\theta_{2}]}\cap\rho\cap\sigma)}}}\right),\>\alpha_{]\theta_{2},\theta_{3}],\sigma,\rho}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{(W(C_{[0,\theta_{3}]})/\nu^{\sigma}(C_{[0,\theta_{2}]}\cap\sigma))\cap\rho\cap\sigma)}}}\right),\\
\alpha_{]\theta_{3},\pi/2],\rho}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\tau^{\rho}(C_{[\theta_{3},\pi/2]}\cap\rho)}}}\right),\>\alpha_{]\theta_{0.8},\theta_{1.2}],\sigma,\rho}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\nu^{\sigma}(C_{]\theta_{0.8},\theta_{1.2}]}\cap\rho\cap\sigma)}}}\right),\\
\alpha_{]\theta_{1.8},\theta_{2.2}],\sigma,\rho}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\nu^{\sigma}(C_{]\theta_{1.8},\theta_{2.2}]}\cap\rho\cap\sigma)}}}\right)
and
\alpha_{]\theta_{2.8},\theta_{3.2}],\sigma,\rho}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\tau^{\rho}(C_{]\theta_{2.8},\theta_{3.2}]}\cap\rho\cap\sigma)}}}\right)
for any
\rho\in\{U,D\}
and
\sigma\in\{L,R\}
(here
\tau^{U}=\tau
,
\tau^{D}=\tau^{-1}
,
\nu^{R}=\nu
and
\nu^{L}=\nu^{-1}
) such that

\alpha=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a}}\right)\circ\bigotimes_{\sigma}\alpha_{[0,\theta_{1}],\sigma}\circ\bigotimes_{\sigma,\rho}\alpha_{]\theta_{1},\theta_{2}],\sigma,\rho}\circ\bigotimes_{\rho}\alpha_{[\theta_{3},\pi/2]}\circ\bigotimes_{\rho,\sigma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha_{]\theta_{0.8},\theta_{1.2}],\rho,\sigma}\otimes\alpha_{]\theta_{1.8},\theta_{2.2}],\rho,\sigma}\otimes\alpha_{]\theta_{2.8},\theta_{3.2}],\rho,\sigma}}}\right).

(14.2)

If additionally everything (except
a
) commutes with
\beta_{g}^{U}
we say that
\alpha\in\textrm{GSQAut}_{1}(\mathcal{A})
.

As a remark, notice that this definition is not identical to the de definition given in
[
10
]
due to the translation operators. Before we prove the theorem, we will first list some required lemmas along with their proof.

Lemma 14.2
.

Take
\omega\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
a short-range entangled state that is
G
-invariant and translation invariant then it satisfies assumption
3.2
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma^{\Phi^{\prime}}_{0;1}
be the disentangler with
\Phi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{F_{\phi}}([0,1])
(for some
0<\phi<1
) a one-parameter family of interactions. By lemma
B.3
part 5 (which is just a slight modification of section 5 from
[
10
]
) we have that
\gamma^{\Phi^{\prime}}_{0;1}\in\textrm{SQAut}_{1}(\mathcal{A})
since
\textrm{SQAut}_{1}(\mathcal{A})\subset\textrm{QAut}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
this implies that
\gamma^{\Phi^{\prime}}_{0;1}\in\textrm{QAut}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
. The existence of the
\tilde{\beta}_{g}
was done in
[
10
]
section 3, where one has to substitute the support of the cones used there by the shifted cones seen in the definition of the
\textrm{SQAut}_{2}
. This concludes the proof that assumption
3.2
is satisfied.
∎

Lemma 14.3
.

Let
\omega
be a short-range entangled state that is
G
-invariant and translation invariant in two directions then it satisfies assumption
3.5
.

Proof.

Completely analogous to the proof of lemma
14.2
.
∎

Lemma 14.4
.

Let
\mu
be the automorphism that rotates every element of the
C^{*}
algebra by
90^{\circ}
degrees, then
\textrm{Index}_{\text{2 trans}}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\omega)=\textrm{Index}_{\text{2 trans}}^{\mathcal{A},U}(\omega\circ\mu)
.

Proof.

This is proven in subsection
10
.
∎

Finally, we prove Theorem
3.8
:

Proof.

The last few lemmas show that the indices are all well-defined. Now we only have to show that they are independent on the choice of representative. We will only prove the first item as all the other proofs are analogous. First notice that the equivalence implies that there exist product states
\phi_{1}\in\mathcal{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{1})
and
\phi_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{2})
and group actions
\tilde{U}_{1}
and
\tilde{U}_{2}
such that the combinations
(\phi_{1},\tilde{U}_{1})
and
(\phi_{2},\tilde{U}_{2})
are trivial (see
2.2
for what this means). Moreover, these will satisfy that there exists a unitary transformation of the on-site Hilbert space,
V
such that

\displaystyle\omega_{1}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\phi_{1}\circ i_{V}

\displaystyle=\omega_{2}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\phi_{2}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;1}

\displaystyle V^{\dagger}U_{1}(g)\otimes\tilde{U}_{1}(g)V

\displaystyle=U_{2}(g)\otimes\tilde{U}_{2}(g)

(14.3)

for some
G
-invariant locally generated automorphism. Using item 1 and item 3 of
3.9
this implies that

\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{1},U_{1}\otimes\tilde{U}_{1}}_{\text{no trans}}(\omega_{1}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\phi_{1})=\textrm{Index}^{\mathcal{A}_{2}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{2},U_{2}\otimes\tilde{U}_{2}}_{\text{no trans}}(\omega_{2}\otimes_{\text{stack}}\phi_{2}).

(14.4)

Using item 2 of
3.9
this is satisfied if
\textrm{Index}^{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{1},\tilde{U}_{1}}_{\text{no trans}}(\phi_{1})=\textrm{Index}^{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{2},\tilde{U}_{2}}_{\text{no trans}}(\phi_{2})=1
. This is trivially true. Only in the last part with the
H^{1}
-valued index does one need that the
(\phi,\tilde{U})
pairs are trivial (see remark
7.1
). For the other three statements, it is enough that we have
G
-invariant product states.
∎

Appendix A
Properties of locally generated automorphisms: 1d

In this section, let
\mathcal{A}
be a quasi-local
C^{*}
algebra over
\mathbb{Z}
.

Lemma A.1
.

Take
\Phi\in\mathcal{B}_{F_{\phi}}([0,1])
(with
F_{\phi}
as presented in (
2.13
)) then there exists an
A\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
satisfying

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A}}\right)=\gamma^{\Phi_{L}+\Phi_{R}}_{0;1}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{1;0}.

(A.1)

Moreover, this
A
is summable (this was defined in subsection
2.1
). More generally there exists a continuous (in norm topology) one-parameter family
\lambda\in[0,1]\mapsto A_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
such that
A_{\lambda}
is summable for each
\lambda
and such that

\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A_{\lambda}}}\right)=\gamma^{\Phi_{L}+\Phi_{R}}_{0;\lambda}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{\lambda;0}.

(A.2)

Proof.

We have for any
B\in\mathcal{A}
that

\displaystyle\gamma^{\Phi_{L}+\Phi_{R}}_{0;t}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{t;0}(B)

\displaystyle=B+\int_{0}^{t}\text{d}s\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}s}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\gamma^{\Phi_{L}+\Phi_{R}}_{0;s}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{s;0}(B)}}\right)

(A.3)

\displaystyle=B-i\int_{0}^{t}\text{d}s\sum_{I\in\mathfrak{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}}\gamma^{\Phi_{L}+\Phi_{R}}_{0;s}([\Phi_{L}(s,I)+\Phi_{R}(s,I)-\Phi(s,I),\gamma^{\Phi}_{s;0}(B)])

(A.4)

\displaystyle=B-i\int_{0}^{t}\text{d}s\gamma^{\Phi_{L}+\Phi_{R}}_{0;s}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{s;0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{I\in\mathfrak{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}}\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;s}(\Phi_{L}(s,I)+\Phi_{R}(s,I)-\Phi(s,I)),B}}\right]}}\right).

(A.5)

To simplify notation we will define an interaction

\tilde{\Phi}(s,I)=\Phi_{L}(s,I)+\Phi_{R}(s,I)-\Phi(s,I).

(A.6)

We will in essence have to find a 1D analogue of the proof of Theorem 5.2 of
[
10
]
with a few exceptions. Most notably we need the summability condition. First, let us still define the extensions

X(m)\vcentcolon=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}|\exists y\in X:\textrm{dist}(x,y)\leq m\}

(A.7)

and

\Delta_{X(m)}\vcentcolon=\Pi_{X(m)}-\Pi_{X(m-1)}

(A.8)

the differences between the conditional expectation values. Define an interaction

\Xi(Z,t)\vcentcolon=\sum_{m\geq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X\subset Z:\\
X(m)=Z\end{subarray}}\Delta_{X(m)}(\gamma^{\Phi}_{0;t}(\tilde{\Phi}(X,t)))

(A.9)

then we get by construction that

\gamma^{\Phi_{L}+\Phi_{R}}_{0;t}\circ\gamma^{\Phi}_{t;0}=\gamma^{\Xi}_{0;t}.

(A.10)

We will now define

A_{\lambda}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{T}\exp(-i\int_0^\lambda\d s\: \sum_{Z\in\mathfrak{B}_{\ZZ}}\Xi(Z,s)).

(A.11)

By construction, if this exists it is continuous in
\lambda
. To show that it exists and is summable, define some sets

\displaystyle B_{n}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=[-n,n]\cap\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle B_{n,L}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=]-\infty,-n]\cap\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle B_{n,R}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=[n,\infty[\cap\mathbb{Z}

(A.12)

and let

A_{n}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{T}\exp(-i\int_0^1 \d s\: \sum_{Z\in\mathfrak{B}_{\ZZ}}\Xi_{B_n}(Z,s)).

(A.13)

In what follows we will find a bound on

c_{n}\vcentcolon=\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\norm{\sum_{Z\in\mathfrak{B}_\ZZ}(\Xi(Z,t)-\Xi_{B_n}(Z,t))}

(A.14)

that is summable. Finding this would conclude the proof because

\sum_{n}\norm{A-A_n}=\sum_{n}\norm{\int_0^1\d s\: \frac{\d}{\d s}A_{n}(s)^\dagger A(s)}\leq\sum_{n}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\norm{\sum_{Z\in\mathfrak{B}_\ZZ}(\Xi(Z,t)-\Xi_{B_n}(Z,t))}=\sum_{n}c_{n}.

(A.15)

In analogy with equation (5.22) of
[
10
]
we get

\displaystyle c_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Z\in\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}:\\
Z\cap B_{n,L}\neq\emptyset\\
\text{or}\\
Z\cap B_{n,R}\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\norm{\Xi(Z,t)}

(A.16)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8}{c_{F}}(e^{2I_{F}(\Phi)}-1)\sum_{m\geq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X\in\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}:\\
X(m)\cap B_{n,L}\neq\emptyset\\
\text{or}\\
X(m)\cap B_{n,R}\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\norm{\tilde{\Phi}(X,t)}}}\right)\absolutevalue{X}G_{F}(m).

(A.17)

Now using a trick analogous to what was done in equation (5.27) of
[
10
]
we get

c_{n}\leq\norm{\Phi_1}_{F}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{m\geq 0}G_{F}(m)}}\right)(\sum_{x\in B_{n,L}}\sum_{y=B_{0,R}}F(d(x,y))+\sum_{x\in B_{0,L}}\sum_{y=B_{n,R}}F(d(x,y))).

(A.18)

To show that the last bound is summable we only have to observe that

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{x\in B_{n,L}}\sum_{y=B_{0,R}}F(d(x,y))\leq\sum_{x\in C_{1}}\sum_{y=C_{2}}F(d(x,y)).

(A.19)

where
C_{1}
is the cone one obtains by putting the
B_{n,L}
on top of each other whereas
C_{2}
is the right half plane of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. The proof that the sum over the cones is bounded is done explicitly in
[
10
]
.
∎

Appendix B
Properties of locally generated automorphisms: 2d

In this section, let
\mathcal{A}
be a quasi-local
C^{*}
algebra over
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Take
H\in\mathcal{B}_{F_{\phi}}([0,1])
(for some
0<\phi<1
) and
\gamma^{H}_{s;t}
its locally generated automorphism (see section
2.3
). We will now show some properties of this locally generated automorphism
\gamma^{H}_{s;t}
. In this section we will heavily rely on the framework and theorems of Yoshiko Ogata
[
10
]
who in her turn relies heavily on the framework developed in
[
8
]
. We now need two additional classes of automorphisms:

Definition B.1
.

Take
\alpha\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
. We say that
\alpha\in\textrm{HAut}_{1}(\mathcal{A})
if and only if for any
0<\theta<\pi/2
there exists an
a\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
and some
\alpha_{\sigma}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\nu^{\sigma}(C_{\theta}\cap\sigma)}}}\right)
for each
\sigma\in\{L,R\}
such that

\alpha=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a}}\right)\circ\alpha_{L}\otimes\alpha_{R}.

(B.1)

Definition B.2
.

Take
\alpha\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{A}}}\right)
. We say that
\alpha\in\textrm{VAut}_{1}(\mathcal{A})
if and only if there exists some
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
\alpha_{U}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau(\mathcal{A}_{C_{\theta}^{c}}\cap\mathcal{A}_{U})}}\right)
and an
\alpha_{D}\in\textrm{Aut}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau^{-1}(\mathcal{A}_{C_{\theta}^{c}}\cap\mathcal{A}_{D})}}\right)
such that

\alpha=\textrm{Ad}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a}}\right)\circ\alpha_{U}\otimes\alpha_{D}.

(B.2)

If furthermore
\alpha_{U}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}=\beta_{g}^{U}\circ\alpha_{U}
we say that
\alpha\in\textrm{GVAut}_{1}(\mathcal{A})
.

Additionally, remember the definition of the
\textrm{QAut}_{2}(\mathcal{A})
that was presented in definition
14.1
. Now we will state some properties of locally generated automorphisms:

Lemma B.3
.

Take
H
an interaction such that there exists a
0<\phi<1
satisfying that
\norm{H}_{f_{\phi}}\leq 1
. The following statements now hold (for any
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
):

1.

\gamma^{H}_{s;t}\circ\gamma^{H_{D}}_{t;s}\otimes\gamma^{H_{U}}_{t;s}\in\textrm{HAut}_{1}(\mathcal{A})
.

2.

\gamma^{H}_{s;t}\circ\gamma^{H_{L}}_{t;s}\otimes\gamma^{H_{R}}_{t;s}\in\textrm{VAut}_{1}(\mathcal{A})
. If additionally
H
is a
G
-invariant interaction we even have
\gamma^{H}_{s;t}\circ\gamma^{H_{L}}_{t;s}\otimes\gamma^{H_{R}}_{t;s}\in\textrm{GVQAut}_{1}(\mathcal{A})
. The similar statement also holds if we replace
L
and
R
by
\nu^{-1}(L)
and
\nu(R)
.

3.

\gamma^{H_{U}}_{s;t}\otimes\gamma^{H_{D}}_{s;t}\in\textrm{SQAut}_{1}(\mathcal{A})
. If additionally
H
is a
G
-invariant interaction we even have
\gamma^{H_{U}}_{s;t}\otimes\gamma^{H_{D}}_{s;t}\in\textrm{GSQAut}_{1}(\mathcal{A})
.

4.

If
H
is
G
-invariant then
\gamma^{H}_{t;s}\circ\beta_{g}^{U}\circ\gamma^{H}_{s;t}\circ(\beta_{g}^{U})^{-1}\in\textrm{HAut}_{1}(\mathcal{A})
.

5.

\gamma^{H}_{t;s}\in\textrm{SQAut}_{1}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

Part 1 is done in Proposition 5.5 in
[
10
]
and Part 4 follows trivially from Part 1. Except for the translation operators in my definition of the
\textrm{SQAut}(\mathcal{A})
, part 3 follows from Theorem 5.2 in
[
10
]
. To show part 3 we therefore have to show that Theorem 5.2 in
[
10
]
still holds if we replace
\mathcal{C}_{0}
and
\mathcal{C}_{1}
in the proof by the sets highlighted in figure
5
. Take the
\Psi
from this proof to be our
H
, take
\Psi^{(0)}
to be
\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{0}}H_{C}
(with our new definition of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
) and take
\Psi^{(1)}\vcentcolon=\Psi-\Psi^{(0)}
. Now define
\Xi^{(s)}(Z,t)
through

\Xi^{(s)}(Z,t)\vcentcolon=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X\subset Z,\\
X(m)=Z\end{subarray}}\Delta_{X(m)}(\gamma^{\Psi}_{s;t}(\Psi^{(1)}(X;t)))

(B.3)

with these new definitions of
\Psi^{(0)}
and
\Psi^{(1)}
. We now want to show that for every
t
,

\sum_{Z\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Xi^{(s)}(Z,t)-\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{1}}\mathbbm{1}_{Z\subset C}\Xi^{(s)}(Z,t)}}\right)

(B.4)

is bounded. Following the arguments in equation (5.22) to (5.24) in
[
10
]
we still get that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Z\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2},\\
\nexists C\in\mathcal{C}_{1}:Z\subset C\end{subarray}}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\norm{\Xi^{(1)}(Z,t)}\leq\frac{8}{C_{F}}(e^{2I_{F}(\Psi)}-1)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C_{1},C_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{0},\\
C_{1}\neq C_{2}\end{subarray}}M(C_{1},C_{2})

(B.5)

where

M(C_{1},C_{2})\vcentcolon=\sum_{m\geq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X:\\
\forall C\in\mathcal{C}_{1},X\cap((C^{c})(m))\neq\emptyset,\\
